Alternatively, we can split the cases of B further for the congruence classes modulo 3 k with > k 1 until the iterated application of B can either be expressed as ↦ + ⁢ a n b + ⁢ c n d with < d c , or it reaches ⊥ . After we perform this procedure while ensuring that the number of newly introduced cases are kept to a minimum, we end up with the following mappings to simulate B in an accelerated manner (similar to the idea in ). ⁢ 9 n ↦ ⁢ 25 n + 16 ⁢ 9 n + 1 ↦ ⁢ 25 n + 21 ⁢ 27 n + 6 ↦ ⁢ 125 n + 64 ⁢ 27 n + 7 ↦ ⁢ 125 n + 71 ⁢ 27 n + 16 ↦ ⁢ 125 n + 114 ⁢ 81 n + 51 ↦ ⁢ 625 n + 459 ⁢ 243 n + 78 ↦ ⁢ 3125 n + 1116 ⁢ 243 n + 159 ↦ ⁢ 3125 n + 2159 Consider the symbols { 0 , 1 , 2 , v , w , x , y , z , ⊲ , ⊳ } with the following functional views. ⁢ 0 ( x ) = ⁢ 3 x ⁢ 1 ( x ) = + ⁢ 3 x 1 ⁢ 2 ( x ) = + ⁢ 3 x 2 v ( x ) = ⁢ 5 x w ( x ) = + ⁢ 5 x 1 x ( x ) = + ⁢ 5 x 2 y ( x ) = + ⁢ 5 x 3 z ( x ) = + ⁢ 5 x 4 ⊲ ( x ) = 0 ⊳ ( x ) = x With these symbols, we have the following mixed { 3 , 5 } -ary (ternary–quinary) system ⁢ B B the termination of which is equivalent to the convergence of B . The dynamic rules on the left implement the mappings from and the rest are auxiliary rules that push ternary symbols towards the rightmost end of the string while preserving its value. We were unable to prove the termination of this system, so we include it as yet another challenge for automated termination proving. 00 ⊳ → yw ⊳ 01 ⊳ → zw ⊳ 020 ⊳ → xxz ⊳ 021 ⊳ → xzw ⊳ 121 ⊳ → zxz ⊳ 1220 ⊳ → yywz ⊳ 02220 ⊳ → wyzyw ⊳ 12220 ⊳ → yxwwz ⊳ ⁢ 0 v → v0 ⁢ 0 w → v1 ⁢ 0 x → v2 ⁢ 0 y → w0 ⁢ 0 z → w1 ⁢ 1 v → w2 ⁢ 1 w → x0 ⁢ 1 x → x1 ⁢ 1 y → x2 ⁢ 1 z → y0 ⁢ 2 v → y1 ⁢ 2 w → y2 ⁢ 2 x → z0 ⁢ 2 y → z1 ⁢ 2 z → z2 ⊲ v → ⊲ 0 ⊲ w → ⊲ 1 ⊲ x → ⊲ 2 ⊲ y → ⊲ 10 ⊲ z → ⊲ 11 C.3 subsection C.3 Section Section Sections Sections C.3 §C.3 C.3Ternary Expansions of 2 n Erdős [Erd79] asked: When does the ternary expansion of 2 n omit the digit 2 ? This is the case for = 2 0 ( 1 ) 3 , = 2 2 ( 11 ) 3 , and = 2 8 ( 100111 ) 3 . He conjectured that it does not happen for > n 8 . This conjecture can be reformulated as the statement that the following rewriting system E , where = ← X { → r ℓ ∣ ℓ → r ∈ X } , is terminating on all initial strings of the form ⊲ ⁢ f 8 f + ⊳ . 0 ⊳ → ⊳ 1 ⊳ → ⊳ ⊲ ⁣ ⊳ → ⊲ ⁣ ⊳ = ← X ⁢ { ⁢ 0 f → f0 ⁢ 0 t → f1 ⁢ 1 f → f2 ⁢ 1 t → t0 ⁢ 2 f → t1 ⁢ 2 t → t2 ⁢ ⊲ t → ⊲ 0 ⊲ ff → ⊲ 1 ⊲ ft → ⊲ 2 } Given a string that corresponds to the binary representation of a power of 2 , the inverted system ← X essentially rewrites the string into ternary by pushing ternary symbols to the right without altering the value that the string represents. The two rules { 0 ⊳ → ⊳ , 1 ⊳ → ⊳ } remove the occurrences of the ternary digits 0 and 1 (but not 2 ). If the ternary expansion does not contain the digit 2 then all digits will be removed, resulting in the string ⊲ ⁣ ⊳ that can then be rewritten to itself indefinitely. This problem, as described, is an instance of “local termination” [WdE10] since it is concerned with termination on not all possible strings but a subset of them. We have not performed experiments with this system or local termination yet and we leave this for future work. .\NiceMatrixBlock[auto-columns-width]\begin{longtable}[]{IJK}{[}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{b}}](\mathbf{x})=\begin{bNiceMatrix}\mathbf{x}&\gg&\begin{bNiceMatrix}\mathbf{x}={[}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{a}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{x}}](\mathbf{x})\\[30.75002pt] {[}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{a}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{a}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{x}}](\mathbf{x})=\begin{bNiceMatrix}\mathbf{x}&\geq&\begin{bNiceMatrix}\mathbf{x}={[}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{b}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{x}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{a}}](\mathbf{x})\\[30.75002pt] {[}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{a}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{b}}](\mathbf{x})=\begin{bNiceMatrix}\mathbf{x}&\geq&\begin{bNiceMatrix}\mathbf{x}={[}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{b}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{a}}](\mathbf{x})\\[30.75002pt] {[}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{x}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{b}}](\mathbf{x})=\begin{bNiceMatrix}\mathbf{x}&\geq&\begin{bNiceMatrix}\mathbf{x}={[}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{a}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{x}}](\mathbf{x})\\[30.75002pt] {[}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{a}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}](\mathbf{x})=\begin{bNiceMatrix}\mathbf{x}&\geq&\begin{bNiceMatrix}\mathbf{x}={[}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{a}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{x}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}](\mathbf{x})\end{example}\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Generalized Collatz Functions} \par We consider instances of the following generalization of the Collatz function. Its variants have commonly appeared in the literature\penalty\ \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{Number}{Lag85,Raw85,Wag85,BM90,Kas92,Koh07,Mic15}{}{}]}. \begin{definition}Let $X$ be one of $\mathbb{N}$, $\mathbb{N}^{+}$, or $\mathbb{Z}$ and define $X_{\bot}\coloneqq X\cup\{\bot\}$. A function $f\colon X_{\bot}\to X_{\bot}$ is a \emph{generalized Collatz function} if $f(\bot)=\bot$ and there exist an integer $d\geq 2$ and rational numbers $q_{0},\dots,q_{d-1},r_{0},\dots,r_{d-1}$ such that for all $0\leq i\leq d-1$ and all $n\in X$, we have a definition \begin{equation*}\begin{aligned} \text{either of the form}\quad&f(n)=q_{i}n+r_{i}&&\text{if }n\equiv i\pmod{d},\\ \text{or of the form}\quad&f(n)=\bot&&\text{if }n\equiv i\pmod{d}.\end{aligned}\end{equation*}\end{definition} In the above definition, we allow the representation of a partially defined function by mapping to $\bot$ in the undefined cases. We call a partial $f$ convergent if all $f$-trajectories contain $\bot$. \par Note that the Collatz function corresponds to the case $d=2$, $q_{0}=1/2$, $r_{0}=0$, $q_{1}=3$, $r_{1}=1$. Although the Collatz function is by far the most widely studied case, there are several other concrete examples of generalized Collatz functions the convergence of which is worth studying due to their connections to open problems in number theory and computability theory. We discuss these cases in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{sec:more-rewriting}. \par\par\@@numbered@section{section}{toc}{Rewriting the Collatz Function} \par We start our discussion with systems that use unary representations and then demonstrate via examples that mixed-base representations can be more suitable for use with automated methods. \par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Rewriting in Unary} \par The following system of Zantema\penalty\ \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{Number}{Zan05}{}{}]} simulates the iterated application of the Collatz function to a number represented in unary, and it terminates upon reaching $1$. \begin{example}$\mathcal{Z}$ denotes the following SRS, consisting of 5 symbols and 7 rules. \begin{equation*}\begin{array}[t]{rcl}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{h}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{h}}\end{array}\qquad\begin{array}[t]{rcl}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{h}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\diamond}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{s}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\diamond}}\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{s}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{s}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\diamond}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{s}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\diamond}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{h}}\end{array}\qquad\begin{array}[t]{rcl}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{h}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\diamond}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\diamond}}\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\diamond}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\diamond}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{h}}\end{array}\end{equation*}\end{example} This system can be seen as encoding the execution of a Turing machine with cells that can be contracted/expanded. The symbols $\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}$ and $\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\diamond}}$ (blank) form the tape alphabet, while the symbols $\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{h}}$ (half), $\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{s}}$ (shift), $\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}$ (triple) indicate the head along with the state of the machine. Through the following result, the Collatz conjecture can be reformulated as termination of string rewriting. \begin{theorem}$\mathcal{Z}$ is terminating if and only if the Collatz conjecture holds. \end{theorem} While the forward direction of the above theorem is easy to see (since $\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\diamond}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{h}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}^{2n}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\diamond}}\to_{\mathcal{Z}}^{*}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\diamond}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{h}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}^{n}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\diamond}}$ for $n>1$ and $\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\diamond}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{h}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}^{2n+1}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\diamond}}\to_{\mathcal{Z}}^{*}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\diamond}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{h}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}^{3n+2}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\diamond}}$ for $n\geq 0$), the backward direction is far from obvious because not every string corresponds to a valid configuration of the underlying machine. \par As another example, consider the system below, which follows a theme similar to \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{ex:zantema-collatz}. \begin{example}$\mathcal{W}$ denotes the following SRS, consisting of 4 symbols and 4 rules. \begin{equation*}\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{h}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{h}}\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{h}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\diamond}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\diamond}}\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\diamond}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\diamond}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{h}}\end{array}\end{equation*}\end{example} Johannes Waldmann shared the above system (originally due to Zantema) with us, mentioning that its termination has yet to be proved via automated methods. Nevertheless, there is a simple reason for its termination: It simulates the iterated application of a partial generalized Collatz function $W\colon\mathbb{N}^{+}_{\bot}\to\mathbb{N}^{+}_{\bot}$ defined as follows, which is easily seen to be convergent. \begin{equation*}W(n)=\begin{cases}3n/2&\text{if }n\equiv 0\pmod{2}\\ \bot&\text{if }n\equiv 1\pmod{2}\end{cases}\end{equation*} \par\par\@@numbered@section{subsubsection}{toc}{Nonexistence of Proofs via Natural Matrix Interpretations} Natural matrix interpretations cannot be used to directly remove any of the rules from the above kind of unary rewriting systems that simulate certain maps, in particular the Collatz function. \begin{definition}A sequence $x_{1},x_{2},\dotsc\in\mathbb{N}$ is called \emph{$\mathbb{N}$-rational} if there exists a matrix $\mathbf{M}\in\mathbb{N}^{d\times d}$ and vectors $\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{N}^{d}$ of some dimension $d$ such that, for all $n$, we have $x_{n}=\mathbf{v}^{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle T}}\mathbf{M}^{n}\mathbf{w}$. \end{definition} \par The following is a consequence of Berstel's theorem\penalty\ \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{Number}{RB10}{}{}, Theorem\penalty\ 8.1.1]}. (See also the remark by Soittola\penalty\ \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{Number}{Soi76}{}{}, page\penalty\ 318]}.) \par\begin{theorem}Let $x_{1},x_{2},\dots$ be an $\mathbb{N}$-rational sequence. Then there exist $m,p\in\mathbb{N}$ such that, for all $j\in\{0,1,\dots,p-1\}$, each of the subsequences $\{x_{m+kp+j}\}_{k=0}^{\infty}$ has its $n$th element equal to $(1+o(1))P(n)\alpha^{n}$ for some nonzero polynomial $P$ and a constant $\alpha>0$. In particular, each of these subsequences is monotonically nondecreasing after some finite point. \end{theorem} \par\begin{corollary}There exists no $\mathbb{N}$-rational sequence $x_{1},x_{2},\dots$ satisfying $x_{8n+1}>x_{9n+2}$ for all $n$. \end{corollary} \@proof[Proof\/\protect\footnotemark] Let $p$ be as in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{thm:berstel}, and consider $n$ of the form $qp-1$ for some large enough $q$. Then $(9n+2)-8n+1=n+1=qp$, so $x_{8n+1}$ and $x_{9n+2}$ belong to the same subsequence. This means that for all sufficiently large $n$ of this form, we must have $x_{8n+1}\leq x_{9n+2}$. \qed\end@proof \par To make use of the above facts in ruling out the existence of natural matrix interpretations of the form described in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{sec:natural-interpretations}, we also need the following result proving that we can work with interpretations that are purely linear (instead of affine) by moving up to a higher dimension. \begin{theorem}Let $R$ be an SRS over the alphabet $\Sigma$, and let $[\sigma](\mathbf{x})=\mathbf{M}_{\sigma}\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{v}_{\sigma}$ denote a $d$-dimensional affine interpretation for each $\sigma\in\Sigma$. Define a $(d+1)$-dimensional linear interpretation for each $\sigma\in\Sigma$ as \begin{equation*}[\sigma]^{+}(\mathbf{x})\coloneqq\mathbf{D}_{\sigma}\mathbf{x}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{M}_{\sigma}&\mathbf{v}_{\sigma}\\ \mathbf{0}^{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle T}}&1\end{bmatrix}\mathbf{x},\end{equation*}where $\mathbf{0}$ denotes the $d$-dimensional zero vector. For each rule $\ell\to r\in R$, let $\mathbf{M}_{\ell}$, $\mathbf{M}_{r}$, $\mathbf{v}_{\ell}$, $\mathbf{v}_{r}$, $\mathbf{D}_{\ell}$, $\mathbf{D}_{r}$ denote the matrices and the vectors occurring in the corresponding interpretations (as obtained by composing the interpretations of the symbols). If we have $\mathbf{M}_{\ell}\gtrsim\mathbf{M}_{r}$ and $\mathbf{v}_{\ell}\gtrsim\mathbf{v}_{r}$, then $\mathbf{D}_{\ell}\gtrsim\mathbf{D}_{r}$. Additionally, if $\mathbf{v}_{\ell}>\mathbf{v}_{r}$, then $(D_{\ell})_{1,d+1}>(D_{r})_{1,d+1}$. \end{theorem} In informal terms, the above theorem states that if we have a collection of affine interpretations to remove a rule from a system (due to the strict decrease across the first elements of the corresponding two vectors), then there is also a collection of purely linear interpretations such that the top-right elements decrease strictly across the two matrices corresponding to this rule. With this fact at hand, we can prove the following. \begin{theorem}Let $\Sigma=\{\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}},\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\diamond}},\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{h}},\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{s}},\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\}$. There exists no collection $[\cdot]_{\Sigma}$ of natural matrix interpretations of any dimension $d$ satisfying the requirements for an extended monotone $\Sigma$-algebra such that \begin{itemize}\itemize@item@for at least one rule $\ell\to r\in\mathcal{Z}$ we have $[\ell](\mathbf{x})>[r](\mathbf{x})$ for all $\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{N}^{d}$, and \par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@for the remaining $\ell^{\prime}\to r^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Z}$ we have $[\ell^{\prime}](\mathbf{x})\gtrsim[r^{\prime}](\mathbf{x})$ for all $\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{N}^{d}$. \end{itemize}\end{theorem} \@proof Assume for a contradiction that such a collection of natural matrix interpretations exists for some dimension $d-1$, with each symbol $\sigma\in\Sigma$ interpreted as $[\sigma](\mathbf{x})=\mathbf{M}_{\sigma}\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{v}_{\sigma}$ and each matrix $\mathbf{D}_{\sigma}$ defined as in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{thm:affine-linear}. Let $\ell\to r\in\mathcal{Z}$ be a rule for which $[\ell](\mathbf{x})>[r](\mathbf{x})$ for all $\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{N}^{d-1}$, and denote $\rho=\{\ell\to r\}$. As the interpretations satisfy the requirements for extended monotonicity, for all $s,t\in\Sigma^{*}$, if $s\to_{\rho}t$, then $[s](\mathbf{x})>[t](\mathbf{x})\text{ for all }\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{N}^{d-1}$. This conclusion implies by\penalty\ \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:matrix-check} that $\mathbf{M}_{s}\gtrsim\mathbf{M}_{t}$ and $\mathbf{v}_{s}>\mathbf{v}_{t}$, which, by \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{thm:affine-linear}, further imply $\mathbf{D}_{s}\gtrsim\mathbf{D}_{t}$ and $(D_{s})_{1,d}>(D_{t})_{1,d}$. Thus, for all $s,t\in\Sigma^{*}$, \begin{equation}s\to_{\rho}t\quad\implies\quad\mathbf{D}_{s}\gtrsim\mathbf{D}_{t}\text{ and }(D_{s})_{1,d}>(D_{t})_{1,d}.\end{equation} \par In Zantema's system $\mathcal{Z}$, we can represent an arbitrary integer $k$ by the string $\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\diamond}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{h}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}^{k}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\diamond}}$, interpreted as \begin{equation*}\big[\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\diamond}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{h}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}^{k}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\diamond}}\big]^{+}(\mathbf{x})=\mathbf{D}_{\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\diamond}}\!\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{h}}}\mathbf{D}_{\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}}^{k}\mathbf{D}_{\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\diamond}}}\mathbf{x}.\end{equation*} Let $\mathbf{e}_{i}\in\mathbb{N}^{d}$ denote the $i$th standard basis vector, i.e., with $1$ at the $i$th position and $0$s elsewhere. Define \@@amsalign where $\mathbf{v}=\mathbf{D}_{\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\diamond}}\!\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{h}}}^{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle T}}\mathbf{e}_{1}$ and $\mathbf{w}=\mathbf{D}_{\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\diamond}}}\mathbf{e}_{d}$ are fixed, so $f(1),f(2),\dots$ is an $\mathbb{N}$-rational sequence. \par Now, since the system $\mathcal{Z}$ simulates the Collatz function (with the odd case incorporating an additional division by $2$), for all numbers of the form $8n+1$ it can simulate the sequence of mappings $8n+1\mapsto 12n+2\mapsto 6n+1\mapsto 9n+2$ that results from applying the Collatz map three times. Thus, for all $n$, we have \begin{equation*}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\diamond}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{h}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}^{8n+1}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\diamond}}\;\to_{\mathcal{Z}}^{*}\;\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\diamond}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{h}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}^{9n+2}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\diamond}},\end{equation*} which crucially requires the use of every single rule in $\mathcal{Z}$ (as it involves applying both $m\mapsto m/2$ and $m\mapsto(3m+1)/2$). This implies in particular that the rule $\ell\to r$ is used in the above derivation. Recall also that the interpretations of all rules in $\mathcal{Z}$ are at least nonstrictly decreasing by assumption. This means along with\penalty\ \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:decrease-rho} that, letting $s=\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\diamond}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{h}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}^{8n+1}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\diamond}}$ and $t=\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\diamond}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{h}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}^{9n+2}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\diamond}}$, we have $(D_{s})_{1,d}>(D_{t})_{1,d}$. From the definition of $f$, for all $n$, \begin{equation*}f(8n+1)=\mathbf{e}_{1}^{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle T}}\mathbf{D}_{s}\mathbf{e}_{d}=(D_{s})_{1,d}>(D_{t})_{1,d}=\mathbf{e}_{1}^{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle T}}\mathbf{D}_{t}\mathbf{e}_{d}=f(9n+2),\end{equation*} but we already argued in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{cor:N-rational-collatz} that no $\mathbb{N}$-rational sequence can satisfy this inequality for all $n$, contradiction. \qed\end@proof \par Natural matrix interpretations fail to be useful for the unary rewriting systems even when used in conjunction with dependency pairs. For the reader familiar with dependency pairs, we show how to adapt the above proof to go through in the setting where the dependency pair transformation is applied before searching for natural matrix interpretations. Recall that, for an SRS $R$ over $\Sigma$, letting $\DP(R)$ denote the SRS consisting of all dependency pairs of $R$ as defined by Arts and Giesl\penalty\ \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{Number}{AG00}{}{}, Definition\penalty\ 3]}, we have $\SN(R)$ if and only if $\SN({\DP(R)}_{\mathrm{top}}\mathbin{/}R)$\penalty\ \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{Number}{AG00}{}{}, Theorem\penalty\ 6]}. For a defined symbol $s\in\Sigma$, we write ${s}_{\#}$ to denote the corresponding tuple symbol. \par Let $\mathcal{I}=\{{\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\diamond}}}_{\#}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{s}}\to{\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\diamond}}}_{\#}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{h}},\ {\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\diamond}}}_{\#}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\to{\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\diamond}}}_{\#}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{h}}\}$, and note that $\mathcal{I}\subseteq\DP(\mathcal{Z})$. With an automated termination prover, the following is straightforward to establish for Zantema's system $\mathcal{Z}$ after applying the dependency pair transformation and performing several steps of rule removal (e.g., using natural matrix interpretations). \par\begin{lemma}$\SN({\DP(\mathcal{Z})}_{\mathrm{top}}\mathbin{/}\mathcal{Z})$ if and only if $\SN({\mathcal{I}}_{\mathrm{top}}\mathbin{/}\mathcal{Z})$. \end{lemma} \par We prove below that neither rule in $\mathcal{I}$ can be removed using natural matrix interpretations. The proof is similar to that of \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{thm:Z-no-direct-natural-matrix}, so we keep it relatively brief. \par\begin{theorem}Let $\Sigma=\{\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}},\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\diamond}},{\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\diamond}}}_{\#},\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{h}},\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{s}},\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\}$. There exists no collection $[\cdot]_{\Sigma}$ of natural matrix interpretations of any dimension $d$ satisfying the requirements for a weakly monotone $\Sigma$-algebra such that \begin{itemize}\itemize@item@for at least one rule $\ell\to r\in\mathcal{I}$ we have $[\ell](\mathbf{x})>[r](\mathbf{x})$ for all $\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{N}^{d}$, and \par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@for every rule $\ell^{\prime}\to r^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Z}$ we have $[\ell^{\prime}](\mathbf{x})\gtrsim[r^{\prime}](\mathbf{x})$ for all $\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{N}^{d}$. \end{itemize}\end{theorem} \@proof Assume for a contradiction that such a collection of natural matrix interpretations exists for some dimension $d-1$, with each symbol $\sigma\in\Sigma$ interpreted as $[\sigma](\mathbf{x})=\mathbf{M}_{\sigma}\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{v}_{\sigma}$ and each matrix $\mathbf{D}_{\sigma}$ defined as in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{thm:affine-linear}. Let $\ell\to r\in\mathcal{I}$ be a rule for which $[\ell](\mathbf{x})>[r](\mathbf{x})$ for all $\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{N}^{d-1}$, and denote $\rho=\{\ell\to r\}$. As the interpretations satisfy the requirements for only weak monotonicity, for all $s,t\in\Sigma^{*}$, if $s\to_{{\rho}_{\mathrm{top}}}t$, then $[s](\mathbf{x})>[t](\mathbf{x})\text{ for all }\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{N}^{d-1}$. Thus, for all $s,t\in\Sigma^{*}$, \begin{equation}s\to_{{\rho}_{\mathrm{top}}}t\quad\implies\quad\mathbf{D}_{s}\gtrsim\mathbf{D}_{t}\text{ and }(D_{s})_{1,d}>(D_{t})_{1,d}.\end{equation} \par We represent an arbitrary integer $k$ by the string ${\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\diamond}}}_{\#}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{h}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}^{k}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\diamond}}$, interpreted as \begin{equation*}\big[{\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\diamond}}}_{\#}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{h}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}^{k}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\diamond}}\big]^{+}(\mathbf{x})=\mathbf{D}_{{\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\diamond}}}_{\#}\!\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{h}}}\mathbf{D}_{\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}}^{k}\mathbf{D}_{\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\diamond}}}\mathbf{x}.\end{equation*} \par Similar to\penalty\ \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:N-rational-interpretation}, we let $\mathbf{e}_{i}\in\mathbb{N}^{d}$ denote the $i$th standard basis vector and define $g(k)\coloneqq\mathbf{u}^{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle T}}\mathbf{D}_{\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}}^{k}\mathbf{w}$, where $\mathbf{u}=\mathbf{D}_{{\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\diamond}}}_{\#}\!\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{h}}}^{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle T}}\mathbf{e}_{1}$ and $\mathbf{w}=\mathbf{D}_{\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\diamond}}}\mathbf{e}_{d}$ are fixed, so $g(1),g(2),\dots$ is an $\mathbb{N}$-rational sequence. \par For all $n$, we have \begin{equation*}{\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\diamond}}}_{\#}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{h}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}^{8n+1}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\diamond}}\;\to_{\mathcal{I}\cup\mathcal{Z}}^{*}\;{\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\diamond}}}_{\#}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{h}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}^{9n+2}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\diamond}},\end{equation*} which crucially requires the use of both rules in $\mathcal{I}$, and, moreover, these rules are applied only at the leftmost end of a string. In particular, the rule $\ell\to r$ is used in the above derivation to perform a top rewrite. Thus, by\penalty\ \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:decrease-rho-top}, for all $n$, we have $g(8n+1)>g(9n+2)$, which contradicts \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{cor:N-rational-collatz}. \qed\end@proof \par Analogues of \lx@cref{creftypeplural\lx@tilde refnum}{thm:Z-no-direct-natural-matrix} and\lx@nobreakspace\lx@cref{refnum}{thm:Z-no-dp-natural-matrix} also hold for the system ${\mathcal{Z}}^{\mathrm{rev}}$, with its rules reversed. Moreover, by arguments similar to above, we can show that natural matrix interpretations fail to prove the termination of $\mathcal{W}$. It is natural to expect that if a proof of the Collatz conjecture is to be produced by some automated method that relies on rewriting, then that method better be able to prove a statement as simple as the convergence of $W$. With this in mind, we describe an alternative rewriting system that simulates the Collatz function and terminates upon reaching $1$. We then provide examples where the alternative system is more suitable for use with termination tools (for instance allowing a matrix interpretations proof of the convergence of $W$). The arguments in this section that prove \lx@cref{creftypeplural\lx@tilde refnum}{thm:Z-no-direct-natural-matrix} and\lx@nobreakspace\lx@cref{refnum}{thm:Z-no-dp-natural-matrix} also appear not to apply to the alternative system, because the connection between rewrite sequences and $\mathbb{N}$-rational sequences gets lost when not using unary representations. More specifically, the proofs crucially use the fact that, since the system $\mathcal{Z}$ represents numbers in unary, it is possible to form a sequence such that the $n$th element is represented by some product $\mathbf{v}^{\mathsf{\scriptscriptstyle T}}\mathbf{M}^{n}\mathbf{w}$, where $\mathbf{v}$ and $\mathbf{w}$ are vectors and $\mathbf{M}$ is a matrix. There is no single such matrix that one can extract when using mixed binary--ternary, or even just binary, representations. Thus, the same kind of analysis, which uses the results known about $\mathbb{N}$-rational sequences, does not go through for the alternative system. \par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Rewriting in Mixed Base} \par In the mixed-base scheme, the overall idea is as follows. Given a number $n\in\mathbb{N}^{+}$, we write a mixed binary--ternary representation for it (noting that this representation is not unique). With this representation, as long as the least significant digit is binary, the parity of the number can be recognized by checking only this digit, as opposed to scanning the entire string when working in unary. This allows us to easily determine the correct case when applying the Collatz function. If the least significant digit is ternary, then the representation is rewritten (while preserving its value) to make this digit binary. Afterwards, since computing $n/2$ corresponds to erasing a trailing binary $0$ and computing $3n+1$ corresponds to inserting a trailing ternary $1$, applying the Collatz function takes a single rewrite step. \par Intuitively, the rewriting system we present can be seen as applying the Collatz map to natural numbers written in binary, with some secondary computation performed using auxiliary symbols to facilitate the application of the map $n\mapsto 3n+1$. However, since termination of rewriting is concerned with sequences that arise from \emph{any initial string} and \emph{any possible application of the rules}, we need to consider the behavior of the system over any string that may be encountered, which includes even the strings that could not result from computing in binary with a prescribed strategy for rule application. The mixed-base scheme is simply a way to give an arithmetical meaning to all strings and all possible rewrite steps. \par More formally, a mixed-base numeral system\penalty\ \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{Number}{Can69}{}{}]} is a numeral system where the base changes across positions, which we define as follows. \begin{definition}Fix $B\subseteq\mathbb{N}$ to be a set of bases. Let $n_{1},\dots,n_{k}\in\mathbb{N}$ and let $b_{1},\dots,b_{k}\in B$. If we have for each $2\leq i\leq k$ that $n_{i}<b_{i}$, then the matrix \begin{equation*}N=\begin{bmatrix}n_{1}&n_{2}&\dots&n_{k}\\ b_{1}&b_{2}&\dots&b_{k}\end{bmatrix}\end{equation*}is called a \emph{mixed $B$-ary representation}. To reduce clutter, we identify the matrix $N$ with a string $(n_{1})_{(b_{1})}(n_{2})_{(b_{2})}\dots(n_{k})_{(b_{k})}$, where each $(n_{i})_{(b_{i})}$ is viewed as a single symbol denoting the \emph{$b_{i}$-ary\penalty\ digit\penalty\ $n_{i}$}. \end{definition} The string $N$ in the above definition represents the number \begin{equation}\Val(N)\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{k}n_{i}\prod_{j=i+1}^{k}b_{j}.\end{equation} Observing that the addition of leading zeros (symbols $0_{b}$ for any $b$) to a string does not change its value, we may assume without loss of generality that $n_{1}>0$. Furthermore, $b_{1}$ does not affect the value of the string, so we replace it by zero (noting that by \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{defn:mixed-base} no other base is zero). \par\begin{example}\begin{enumerate}\enumerate@item@With $B=\{2\}$, we have the binary numeral system. In the notation of \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{defn:mixed-base} we write $1_{2}0_{2}1_{2}1_{2}$ to represent the number $11$. \par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@With $B=\{2,3\}$, we obtain the mixed binary--ternary system that we will use in the rest of this paper. In this system we may write, for instance, $1_{0}2_{3}1_{2}$ or $2_{0}1_{2}1_{2}$ or $1_{0}1_{2}2_{3}$ to represent the number $11$. \end{enumerate}\end{example} \par Now, define $\beta_{b}^{n}(x)\coloneqq bx+n$. After rearranging\penalty\ \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:value-string}, we see that for a mixed $B$-ary string $N=(n_{1})_{(b_{1})}(n_{2})_{(b_{2})}\dots(n_{k})_{(b_{k})}$, its value $\Val(N)$ is also given by evaluating the composite function \begin{equation}\Gamma_{N}(x)\coloneqq(\beta_{b_{k}}^{n_{k}}\circ\beta_{b_{k-1}}^{n_{k-1}}\circ\dots\circ\beta_{b_{1}}^{n_{1}})(x)\end{equation} at any value (because $b_{1}=0$ implies that the innermost function $\beta_{0}^{n_{1}}(x)=n_{1}$ is constant). This gives us a string and a function view of the same representation, and we will switch between them as appropriate. In doing so, we also conflate the symbols and the corresponding functions, referring to $\beta_{b}^{n}$ as $n_{b}$. \par As the last ingredient before describing the rewriting system, we observe that we can write $(\beta_{b}^{n}\circ\beta_{c}^{m})(x)=bcx+bm+n$ equivalently as another composition $(\beta_{c}^{m^{\prime}}\circ\beta_{b}^{n^{\prime}})(x)=cbx+cn^{\prime}+m^{\prime}$ for some suitable $0\leq n^{\prime}<b$ and $0\leq m^{\prime}<c$. This allows us to swap the bases of adjacent positions while preserving the value of the string. \par From this point on, we constrain ourselves to the mixed $\{2,3\}$-ary (binary--ternary) representations as we shift our focus to simulating the Collatz function (noting that it is possible to adapt the resulting rewriting system for other instances of the general case). More precisely, we simulate the following redefinition of the Collatz function, where the odd case incorporates an additional division by $2$. \begin{equation*}T(n)=\begin{cases}\frac{n}{2}&\text{if }n\equiv 0\pmod{2}\\ \frac{3n+1}{2}&\text{if }n\equiv 1\pmod{2}\end{cases}\end{equation*} \par We will describe an SRS $\mathcal{T}$ over the symbols $\{\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}},\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}},\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}},\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}},\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}},\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}},\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}\}$ that simulates the iterated application of the Collatz function and terminates upon reaching $1$. The symbols $\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}},\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}$ correspond to binary digits $0_{2},1_{2}$; and $\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}},\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}},\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}}$ to ternary digits $0_{3},1_{3},2_{3}$. The symbol $\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}$ marks the beginning of a string while also standing for the most significant digit (without loss of generality assumed to be $1_{0}$) and $\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}$ marks the end of a string while also standing for the redundant trailing digit $0_{1}$. Consider the functional view of these symbols: \begin{equation}\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}(x)&=&2x\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}(x)&=&2x+1\end{array}\qquad\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}(x)&=&3x\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}(x)&=&3x+1\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}}(x)&=&3x+2\end{array}\qquad\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}(x)&=&1\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}(x)&=&x\end{array}\end{equation} Each positive integer can be expressed as some composition of these functions, which corresponds to a string as per our previous discussion. \par\begin{example}Viewing the expression $\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}(x)$ as the constant $1$, we can write \begin{equation*}19=\Val(\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}})=\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}(\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}(\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}(\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}(\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}(x))))).\end{equation*}The string representation ends with a ternary symbol, so we will rewrite it. \par With the function view, we have \begin{equation*}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}(\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}(x))=3(2x)+1=6x+1=2(3x)+1=\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}(\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}(x)).\end{equation*}This shows that we could also write $19=\Val(\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}})$, which now ends with the binary digit $1_{2}$. This gives us the rewrite rule $\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}\to\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}$. We can now apply the Collatz function to this representation by rewriting only the rightmost two symbols of the string since \begin{equation*}T(\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}(\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}(x)))=\frac{3(2x+1)+1}{2}=\frac{6x+4}{2}=3x+2=(\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}(\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}}(x))).\end{equation*}This gives us the rewrite rule $\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}\to\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}$. After applying this rule to the string $\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}$, we indeed obtain $T(19)=29=\Val(\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}})$. \end{example} \par In the manner of the above example, we compute all the necessary transformations and obtain the following 11-rule SRS $\mathcal{T}$. \begin{equation*}\mathcal{D}_{T}=\left\{\begin{array}[c]{rcl}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}\end{array}\right\}\qquad\mathcal{A}=\left\{\begin{array}[c]{rcl}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\end{array}\qquad\begin{array}[c]{rcl}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\end{array}\right\}\qquad\mathcal{B}=\left\{\begin{array}[c]{rcl}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\end{array}\right\}\end{equation*} This SRS is split into subsystems $\mathcal{D}_{T}$ (dynamic rules for $T$) and $\mathcal{X}=\mathcal{A}\cup\mathcal{B}$ (auxiliary rules). The two rules in $\mathcal{D}_{T}$ encode the application of the Collatz function $T$, while the rules in $\mathcal{X}$ serve to push binary symbols towards the rightmost end of the string by swapping the bases of adjacent positions without changing the represented value. \par\begin{example}Consider the string $s=\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}$, which represents the number $12$. Below is a possible rewrite sequence of $\mathcal{T}$ that starts from $s$, with the corresponding values (under the interpretations from\penalty\ \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:interp}) displayed above the strings. Underlines indicate the parts of the strings where the rules are applied. \begin{equation*}\begin{array}[c]{*{16}{@{\,}c@{\,}}}&12&&12&&6&&6&&3&&3&&5&&5\\[2.5pt] &\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\underline{\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}&\to_{\mathcal{A}}&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}\underline{\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}}&\to_{\mathcal{D}_{T}}&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}\underline{\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}&\to_{\mathcal{A}}&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}\underline{\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}}&\to_{\mathcal{D}_{T}}&\underline{\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}&\to_{\mathcal{B}}&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}\underline{\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}}&\to_{\mathcal{D}_{T}}&\underline{\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}&\to_{\mathcal{B}}&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\underline{\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}}\\[10.00002pt] &8&&8&&8&&4&&2&&1\\[2.5pt] \to_{\mathcal{D}_{T}}&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}\underline{\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}&\to_{\mathcal{A}}&\underline{\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}&\to_{\mathcal{B}}&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\underline{\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}}&\to_{\mathcal{D}_{T}}&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\underline{\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}}&\to_{\mathcal{D}_{T}}&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}\underline{\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}}&\to_{\mathcal{D}_{T}}&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}\end{array}\end{equation*}\end{example} The trajectory of $T$ would continue upon reaching $1$; however, in order to be able to formulate the Collatz conjecture as a termination problem, $\mathcal{T}$ is made in such a way that its rewrite sequences stop upon reaching the string representation $\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}$ of $1$ since no rule is applicable. \par Termination of the subsystems of $\mathcal{T}$ with $\mathcal{B}$ or $\mathcal{D}_{T}$ removed is easily seen. However, since we have matrix interpretations at our disposal, let us give a compact and formal proof. \begin{lemma}$\SN(\mathcal{T}\setminus\mathcal{B})$ and $\SN(\mathcal{T}\setminus\mathcal{D}_{T})$. \end{lemma} \@proof It is easily checked that the interpretations below show $\SN({(\mathcal{T}\setminus\mathcal{B})}^{\mathrm{rev}})$, which implies $\SN(\mathcal{T}\setminus\mathcal{B})$ by \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:reversal}. \begin{equation*}[\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}](x)=[\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}](x)=2x+1\qquad[\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}]=x\qquad[\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}](x)=[\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}](x)=[\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}}](x)=2x\end{equation*} \par Similarly, the below interpretations show $\SN({(\mathcal{T}\setminus\mathcal{D}_{T})}^{\mathrm{rev}})$, which implies $\SN(\mathcal{T}\setminus\mathcal{D}_{T})$ by \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:reversal}. \begin{equation*}[\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}](x)=[\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}](x)=[\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}](x)=x+1\qquad[\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}](x)=[\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}](x)=[\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}}](x)=4x\qed\end{equation*} \end@proof \par When considering the termination of $\mathcal{T}$ (on all initial strings), it suffices to limit the discussion to initial strings of a specific form that we have been working with so far, e.g., in \lx@cref{creftypeplural\lx@tilde refnum}{ex:sample-rewrite-T} and\lx@nobreakspace\lx@cref{refnum}{ex:rewrite-sequence-T}. \begin{lemma}If $\mathcal{T}$ is terminating on all initial strings of the \emph{canonical form} $\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}(\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}|\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}|\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}|\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}|\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}})^{*}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}$, then $\mathcal{T}$ is terminating (on all initial strings). \end{lemma} For the proof of \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:blockwise-termination}, we will use \emph{type introduction}, which is a technique for proving termination by switching to typed rewriting. We refer the reader to Sabel\penalty\ and\penalty\ Zantema\penalty\ \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{Number}{SZ17}{}{}, Section\penalty\ 3.1]} for background. We also offer a more elementary proof in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{sec:alternative-proof-blockwise-termination}. \@proof[Proof of \cref{lem:blockwise-termination}] Let $\mathbb{T}=\{\rho,\sigma,\tau\}$ be a set of types. It is straightforward to see that, with respect to the typing \begin{equation*}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}:\sigma\to\tau,\qquad\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}},\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}},\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}},\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}},\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}}:\sigma\to\sigma,\qquad\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}:\rho\to\sigma,\end{equation*} the SRS $\mathcal{T}$ is well-typed, so it is terminating (in the untyped setting) if and only if it is terminating in the typed setting\penalty\ \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{Number}{SZ17}{}{}, Corollary\penalty\ 3.4]}. Due to \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:A-termination}, a well-typed string with $\sigma$ as its source or target type cannot admit an infinite rewrite sequence for $\mathcal{T}$. Thus, a well-typed string admits an infinite rewrite sequence only if it is of type $\rho\to\tau$, which implies that it is of the canonical form. \qed\end@proof Note that the converse of the above lemma is obvious, since if $\mathcal{T}$ is terminating then it is terminating on all initial strings of any form, in particular $\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}(\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}|\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}|\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}|\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}|\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}})^{*}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}$. \par As a whole, the rewriting system $\mathcal{T}$ simulates the iterated application of $T$ (except at $1$). \begin{theorem}$\mathcal{T}$ is terminating if and only if $T$ is convergent (i.e., the Collatz conjecture holds). \end{theorem} \@proof Without loss of generality (due to \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:blockwise-termination}), consider only the strings of the canonical form $\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}(\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}|\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}|\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}|\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}|\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}})^{*}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}$ throughout this proof. We will show that, given such a string $N$, all rewrite sequences for $\mathcal{T}$ that start from $N$ simulate the $T$-trajectory of $\Val(N)$, except when $\Val(N)=1$. More specifically, we prove the following and use them to deduce the theorem statement. \begin{claim}For any pair $N,N^{\prime}$ of strings, if $N\to_{\mathcal{X}}N^{\prime}$ then $\Val(N^{\prime})=\Val(N)$, and if $N\to_{\mathcal{D}_{T}}N^{\prime}$ then $\Val(N^{\prime})=T(\Val(N))$. \end{claim} \begin{claim}For any string $N$ with $\Val(N)=1$, there exists no string $N^{\prime}$ satisfying $N\to_{\mathcal{T}}N^{\prime}$. \end{claim} \begin{claim}For any integer $\nu>1$ and any string $N$ with $\Val(N)=\nu$, there exists a string $N^{\prime}$ such that $N\to_{\mathcal{T}}^{*}N^{\prime}$ and $\Val(N^{\prime})=T(\nu)$. \end{claim} \@proof[Proof of \cref{claim:equiv-rewrite-num}] Assume $N\to_{\mathcal{X}}N^{\prime}$. Then there exist strings $X,Y$ and some rule $\ell\to r\in\mathcal{X}$ such that $N=X\ell Y$ and $N^{\prime}=XrY$. Recalling the definition of $\Gamma$ from\penalty\ \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:comp-string}, we have $\Val(N^{\prime})=\Val(N)$ if and only if $\Gamma_{Y}\circ\Gamma_{r}\circ\Gamma_{X}=\Gamma_{Y}\circ\Gamma_{\ell}\circ\Gamma_{X}$ for all $\ell\to r\in\mathcal{X}$. This holds if and only if $\Gamma_{r}=\Gamma_{\ell}$ for all $\ell\to r\in\mathcal{X}$. With the functional views of the symbols as in\penalty\ \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:interp}, we indeed have: \begin{equation*}\begin{array}[]{lclcccrcr}\Gamma_{\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}\!\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}}(x)&=&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}(\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}(x))&=&6x&=&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}(\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}(x))&=&\Gamma_{\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\!\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}}(x)\\[2.5pt] \Gamma_{\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}\!\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}}(x)&=&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}(\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}(x))&=&6x+1&=&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}(\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}(x))&=&\Gamma_{\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\!\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}}(x)\\[2.5pt] \Gamma_{\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}\!\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}}(x)&=&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}(\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}(x))&=&6x+2&=&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}}(\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}(x))&=&\Gamma_{\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\!\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}}}(x)\\[2.5pt] \Gamma_{\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}\!\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}}(x)&=&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}(\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}(x))&=&6x+3&=&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}(\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}(x))&=&\Gamma_{\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\!\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}}(x)\\[2.5pt] \Gamma_{\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}}\!\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}}(x)&=&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}(\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}}(x))&=&6x+4&=&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}(\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}(x))&=&\Gamma_{\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\!\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}}(x)\\[2.5pt] \Gamma_{\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}}\!\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}}(x)&=&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}(\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}}(x))&=&6x+5&=&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}}(\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}(x))&=&\Gamma_{\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\!\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}}}(x)\\[2.5pt] \Gamma_{\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}\!\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}}(x)&=&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}(\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}(x))&=&3&=&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}(\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}(x))&=&\Gamma_{\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}\!\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}}(x)\\[2.5pt] \Gamma_{\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}\!\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\!\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}}(x)&=&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}(\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}(\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}(x)))&=&4&=&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}(\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}(x))&=&\Gamma_{\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}\!\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}}(x)\\[2.5pt] \Gamma_{\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}\!\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\!\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}}(x)&=&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}(\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}(\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}(x)))&=&5&=&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}}(\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}(x))&=&\Gamma_{\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}\!\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}}}(x)\end{array}\end{equation*} This proves the first part of the claim. \par Now assume $N\to_{\mathcal{D}_{T}}N^{\prime}$. Again, there exist a string $X$ and some rule $\ell\to r\in\mathcal{D}_{T}$ such that $N=X\ell$ and $N^{\prime}=Xr$ (since the rules in $\mathcal{D}_{T}$ can be applied only at the rightmost end of the string). We have $\Val(N^{\prime})=T(\Val(N))$ if and only if $\Gamma_{r}\circ\Gamma_{X}=T\circ\Gamma_{\ell}\circ\Gamma_{X}$ for all $\ell\to r\in\mathcal{D}_{T}$. This holds if and only if $\Gamma_{r}=T\circ\Gamma_{\ell}$ for all $\ell\to r\in\mathcal{D}_{T}$. There are only two rules to check, and indeed we have: \begin{equation*}\begin{array}[]{lclcccccrcr}\Gamma_{\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}}(x)&=&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}(x)&=&x&=&T(2x)&=&T(\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}(\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}(x)))&=&T(\Gamma_{\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\!\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}}(x))\\[2.5pt] \Gamma_{\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}}\!\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}}(x)&=&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}(\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}}(x))&=&3x+2&=&T(2x+1)&=&T(\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}(\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}(x)))&=&T(\Gamma_{\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\!\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}}(x))\end{array}\end{equation*} This proves the second part of the claim. \qed\end@proof \@proof[Proof of \cref{claim:equiv-1}] The string $\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}$ has $\Val(\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}})=1$, and inserting any symbol from $\{\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}},\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}},\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}},\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}},\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}}\}$ in between the delimiters increases the value of the string, so $\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}$ is the unique string representing $1$. None of the rules in $\mathcal{T}$ apply to $\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}$, which proves the claim. \qed\end@proof \@proof[Proof of \cref{claim:equiv-num-rewrite}] Let $\nu>1$ be an integer, and let $N$ be a string with $\Val(N)=\nu$. We will describe a rewrite sequence $N\to_{\mathcal{X}}^{*}N^{\prime}\to_{\mathcal{D}_{T}}N^{\prime\prime}$, which implies by \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{claim:equiv-rewrite-num} that $\Val(N^{\prime\prime})=T(\nu)$. For the rest of the proof let $b_{i}$ and $t_{i}$ stand for binary and ternary symbols, respectively. Assume that $N$ contains some binary symbol ($\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}$ or $\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}$), since otherwise it is of the form $\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}(\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}|\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}|\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}})^{*}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}$ and we can apply some rule from $\mathcal{B}$ to produce a binary symbol. Then the string contains some substring $b_{1}t_{2}t_{3}\dots t_{k}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}$. We rewrite this substring into $t_{1}^{\prime}b_{2}t_{3}\dots t_{k}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}$ by applying some rule from $\mathcal{X}$. For each $1<i<k$, assuming the current string contains some substring $b_{i}t_{i+1}\dots t_{k}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}$, we rewrite it into $t_{i}^{\prime}b_{i+1}t_{i+2}\dots t_{k}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}$. In the end we obtain a string $N^{\prime}$ with $\Val(N^{\prime})=\nu$ that contains some $b_{k}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}$, so we apply some rule from $\mathcal{D}_{T}$ to obtain $N^{\prime\prime}$ such that $\Val(N^{\prime\prime})=T(\nu)$. \qed\end@proof \par Assume that the SRS $\mathcal{T}$ is not terminating. Let $(N_{i})_{i=0}^{\infty}$ be an infinite rewrite sequence for $\mathcal{T}$, and consider the sequence $(\nu_{i})_{i=0}^{\infty}$, where $\nu_{i}=\Val(N_{i})$. By \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{claim:equiv-rewrite-num}, for all $i\in\mathbb{N}$, we have either $\nu_{i+1}=\nu_{i}$ or $\nu_{i+1}=T(\nu_{i})$ depending on the rewrite rule used. Let $I=\{i\in\mathbb{N}\mid\nu_{i+1}=T(\nu_{i})\}$, which is the same as the set of indices at which some rule from $\mathcal{D}_{T}$ is applied in the rewrite sequence. By \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:A-termination}, the subsystem $\mathcal{X}=\mathcal{T}\setminus\mathcal{D}_{T}$ is terminating, so the rewrite $\mathord{\to}_{\mathcal{D}_{T}}$ is performed infinitely many times in the sequence. This implies that $I$ is infinite, proving that the sequence $(\nu_{i})_{i\in I}$ is a Collatz trajectory. Moreover, this trajectory is nonconvergent, i.e., it does not contain $1$, since otherwise the rewrite sequence could not be infinite (because of \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{claim:equiv-1}). This proves the backward implication in the theorem statement. \par Assume that the function $T$ is not convergent. Let $(\nu_{i})_{i=0}^{\infty}$ be a nonconvergent trajectory, and note that $\nu_{i}>1$ for all $i\in\mathbb{N}$. We will inductively define a sequence $(N_{i})_{i=0}^{\infty}$ such that $\Val(N_{i})=\nu_{i}$ for all $i\in\mathbb{N}$. Let $N_{0}$ be any string with $\Val(N_{0})=\nu_{0}$. For each $i\in\mathbb{N}^{+}$, assuming $\Val(N_{i-1})=\nu_{i-1}$, let $N_{i}$ be a string such that $N_{i-1}\to_{\mathcal{T}}^{*}N_{i}$ and $\Val(N_{i})=T(\nu_{i-1})=\nu_{i}$, which exists by \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{claim:equiv-num-rewrite}. Defined in this manner, the sequence $(N_{i})_{i=0}^{\infty}$ satisfies $N_{i}\to_{\mathcal{T}}^{*}N_{i+1}$ for all $i\in\mathbb{N}$, meaning that $\mathcal{T}$ is not terminating. This proves the forward implication in the theorem statement, which concludes the proof. \qed\end@proof \par When trying to remove a rule in $\mathcal{D}_{T}$ or $\mathcal{B}$ it suffices to show relative top termination, allowing us to use weakly (instead of extended) monotone algebras when applying \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{thm:monotone-algebra} and take advantage of the more relaxed constraints when searching for matrix interpretations. As we mentioned back in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{sec:string-rewriting-systems}, the lemma below encapsulates dependency pairs, and it can in fact be automatically proved by the existing termination tools via the dependency pair transformation followed by an application of the dependency pair framework\penalty\ \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{Number}{GTSF06}{}{}]}. \begin{lemma}\begin{enumerate}\enumerate@item@For each subset $\mathcal{R}\subseteq\mathcal{B}$, if $\SN(\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{top}}\mathbin{/}\mathcal{T})$ then $\SN(\mathcal{R}\mathbin{/}\mathcal{T})$. \par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@For each subset $\mathcal{Q}\subseteq\mathcal{D}_{T}$, if $\SN({\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{rev}}_{\mathrm{top}}\mathbin{/}{\mathcal{T}}^{\mathrm{rev}})$ then $\SN({\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{rev}}\mathbin{/}{\mathcal{T}}^{\mathrm{rev}})$. \end{enumerate}\end{lemma} \@proof Without loss of generality (due to \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:blockwise-termination}), consider only the rewrite sequences that start with some string of the canonical form $\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}(\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}|\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}|\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}|\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}|\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}})^{*}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}$ (resp.\ its reversal). \par Let $\mathcal{R}\subseteq\mathcal{B}$ (resp.\ $\mathcal{Q}\subseteq\mathcal{D}_{T}$). The rules in $\mathcal{R}$ (resp.\ ${\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{rev}}$) can be applied only at the leftmost end as they are all of the form $\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{s}\to\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{t}$ for some $s$, $t$ not containing $\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}$ (resp.\ $\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}\mathmakebox[.67em]{s^{\prime}}\to\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}\mathmakebox[.67em]{t^{\prime}}$ for some $s^{\prime}$, $t^{\prime}$ not containing $\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}$). \par Assume $\neg\SN(\mathcal{R}\mathbin{/}\mathcal{T})$ (resp.\ $\neg\SN({\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{rev}}\mathbin{/}{\mathcal{T}}^{\mathrm{rev}})$). Then there exists an infinite rewrite sequence for $\mathcal{T}$ (resp. ${\mathcal{T}}^{\mathrm{rev}}$) where the rules from $\mathcal{R}$ (resp.\ ${\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{rev}}$) are applied infinitely many times. As these rules can be applied only at the leftmost end, this implies relative top nontermination, i.e., $\neg\SN(\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{top}}\mathbin{/}\mathcal{T})$ (resp.\ $\neg\SN({\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{rev}}_{\mathrm{top}}\mathbin{/}{\mathcal{T}}^{\mathrm{rev}})$). \qed\end@proof \par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Rewriting with a Hybrid System} \par As another alternative to the rewriting system $\mathcal{Z}$, instead of fully discarding unary in favor of a positional numeral system we may adopt a hybrid between the two approaches and design a system to increment/decrement by $1$ or multiply/divide by $2$ or $3$ in a single rewrite. We will describe an SRS over the symbols $\{\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{i}},\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}},\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}},\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}},\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}\}$ viewed as follows. \begin{equation*}\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{i}}(x)&=&x+1\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}(x)&=&2x\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}(x)&=&3x\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}(x)&=&1\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}(x)&=&x\end{array}\end{equation*} In essentially the same manner as we did for the system $\mathcal{T}$, we have a set of dynamic rules that simulate the application of the Collatz function and a set of auxiliary rules that update the string representation so that the parity of the corresponding number can be recognized in a single step. We have the following 7-rule SRS $\mathcal{L}$ (designed by Luke Schaeffer). \begin{equation*}\mathcal{D}_{T}^{\prime}=\left\{\begin{array}[c]{rcl}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{i}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{i}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{i}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}\end{array}\right\}\quad\mathcal{A}^{\prime}=\left\{\begin{array}[c]{rcl}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{i}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{i}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{i}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{i}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{i}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{i}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{i}}\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\end{array}\right\}\quad\mathcal{B}^{\prime}=\left\{\begin{array}[c]{rcl}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{i}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{i}}\\ \end{array}\right\}\end{equation*} As long as the string ends with $\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}$ or $\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{i}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}$, the parity of the represented number is apparent without needing to scan the whole string, which allows the Collatz function to be applied in a single rewrite. After the application of a dynamic rule, the string may no longer end with $\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}$ or $\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{i}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}$, so the auxiliary rules in $\mathcal{X}^{\prime}=\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\cup\mathcal{B}^{\prime}$ push the symbol $\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}$ towards the rightmost end of the string while preserving its value. Thus, $\mathcal{L}$ is yet another rewriting system that simulates the iterated application of the Collatz function. \begin{theorem}$\mathcal{L}$ is terminating if and only if $T$ is convergent. \end{theorem} The proof of the above theorem is essentially the same as that of \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{thm:simulation} once it is checked that the dynamic rules do indeed apply $T$ and that the auxiliary rules do indeed preserve the value of a string under the functional view of the symbols. \par\par\@@numbered@section{section}{toc}{Automated Proofs} \par We adapt the rewriting system $\mathcal{T}$ for different generalized Collatz functions to explore the effectiveness of the mixed-base scheme on weakened variants of the Collatz conjecture. \par All of the rewriting systems, scripts to reproduce the proofs, and our minimal implementation of a termination prover are available at \url{https://github.com/emreyolcu/rewriting-collatz}. In particular, any omitted relative termination proof that we refer to in this section is available at \url{https://github.com/emreyolcu/rewriting-collatz/tree/main/proofs}. We ran the large-scale experiments in this section on AWS EC2 {m5.metal} instances. \par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{A Simple Example} \par Earlier we mentioned a generalized Collatz function $W$ as a simple example that could serve as a sanity check for an automated method aiming to solve Collatz-like problems. With the mixed binary--ternary scheme, this function can be seen to be simulated by the system $\mathcal{W}^{\prime}=\{\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}\to\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}\}\cup\mathcal{X}$. A small matrix interpretations proof is found for this system in less than a second, in contrast to its variant $\mathcal{W}$ that uses unary representations for which no automated proof is known. Additionally, the function $W$ can be simulated by the rewriting system $\mathcal{W}^{\prime\prime}=\{\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}\to\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}\}\cup\mathcal{X}^{\prime}$ adapted from the hybrid system $\mathcal{L}$, although we were unable to find an automated proof of termination for $\mathcal{W}^{\prime\prime}$. \par\begin{theorem}$\SN(\mathcal{W}^{\prime})$. \end{theorem} \@proof The interpretations below prove $\SN(\{\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\to\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}\}\mathbin{/}{\mathcal{X}}^{\mathrm{rev}})$: { \@@amsgather }With the above interpretations of the symbols, the rules of ${\mathcal{W}^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{rev}}$ satisfy the following relations, which, by \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{thm:monotone-algebra}, proves that $\{\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\to\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}\}$ is terminating relative to ${\mathcal{X}}^{\mathrm{rev}}$. \begin{longtable}[]{IJK}{[}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}](\mathbf{x})=\begin{bmatrix}1&2\\ 0&0\end{bmatrix}\mathbf{x}+\begin{bmatrix}3\\ 0\end{bmatrix}&>&\begin{bmatrix}1&2\\ 0&0\end{bmatrix}\mathbf{x}+\begin{bmatrix}2\\ 0\end{bmatrix}={[}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}](\mathbf{x})\\[18.24998pt] {[}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}](\mathbf{x})=\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\ 0&1\end{bmatrix}\mathbf{x}+\begin{bmatrix}3\\ 1\end{bmatrix}&\gtrsim&\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\ 0&1\end{bmatrix}\mathbf{x}+\begin{bmatrix}3\\ 1\end{bmatrix}={[}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}](\mathbf{x})\\[18.24998pt] {[}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}](\mathbf{x})=\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\ 1&0\end{bmatrix}\mathbf{x}+\begin{bmatrix}3\\ 3\end{bmatrix}&\gtrsim&\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\ 0&0\end{bmatrix}\mathbf{x}+\begin{bmatrix}3\\ 0\end{bmatrix}={[}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}](\mathbf{x})\\[18.24998pt] {[}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}](\mathbf{x})=\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\ 1&0\end{bmatrix}\mathbf{x}+\begin{bmatrix}3\\ 3\end{bmatrix}&\gtrsim&\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\ 1&0\end{bmatrix}\mathbf{x}+\begin{bmatrix}3\\ 3\end{bmatrix}={[}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}](\mathbf{x})\\[18.24998pt] {[}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}](\mathbf{x})=\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\ 0&0\end{bmatrix}\mathbf{x}+\begin{bmatrix}3\\ 0\end{bmatrix}&\gtrsim&\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\ 0&0\end{bmatrix}\mathbf{x}+\begin{bmatrix}3\\ 0\end{bmatrix}={[}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}](\mathbf{x})\\[18.24998pt] {[}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}](\mathbf{x})=\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\ 1&0\end{bmatrix}\mathbf{x}+\begin{bmatrix}3\\ 3\end{bmatrix}&\gtrsim&\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\ 1&0\end{bmatrix}\mathbf{x}+\begin{bmatrix}3\\ 3\end{bmatrix}={[}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}}](\mathbf{x})\\[18.24998pt] {[}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}](\mathbf{x})=\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\ 1&0\end{bmatrix}\mathbf{x}+\begin{bmatrix}3\\ 3\end{bmatrix}&\gtrsim&\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\ 0&0\end{bmatrix}\mathbf{x}+\begin{bmatrix}3\\ 0\end{bmatrix}={[}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}}](\mathbf{x})\\[18.24998pt] {[}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}](\mathbf{x})=\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\ 0&0\end{bmatrix}\mathbf{x}+\begin{bmatrix}2\\ 0\end{bmatrix}&>&\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\ 0&0\end{bmatrix}\mathbf{x}+\begin{bmatrix}1\\ 0\end{bmatrix}={[}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}](\mathbf{x})\\[18.24998pt] {[}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}](\mathbf{x})=\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\ 1&0\end{bmatrix}\mathbf{x}+\begin{bmatrix}2\\ 2\end{bmatrix}&\gtrsim&\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\ 0&0\end{bmatrix}\mathbf{x}+\begin{bmatrix}2\\ 2\end{bmatrix}={[}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}](\mathbf{x})\\[18.24998pt] {[}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}](\mathbf{x})=\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\ 1&0\end{bmatrix}\mathbf{x}+\begin{bmatrix}2\\ 2\end{bmatrix}&\gtrsim&\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\ 0&0\end{bmatrix}\mathbf{x}+\begin{bmatrix}2\\ 0\end{bmatrix}={[}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}](\mathbf{x})\end{longtable} \noindent By \lx@cref{creftypeplural\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:A-termination} and\lx@nobreakspace\lx@cref{refnum}{lem:reversal}, ${\mathcal{X}}^{\mathrm{rev}}$ is terminating. As a result, ${\mathcal{W}^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{rev}}$ is terminating, which by \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:reversal} implies that $\mathcal{W}^{\prime}$ is terminating. \qed\end@proof \par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Farkas' Variant} \par Let ${2\mathbb{N}+1}=\{1,3,5,\dots\}$ denote the odd natural numbers. Farkas\penalty\ \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{Number}{Far05}{}{}]} studied a slight modification $F^{\prime}\colon{2\mathbb{N}+1}\to{2\mathbb{N}+1}$ of the Collatz function for which it becomes possible to prove convergence via induction. We consider automatically proving the convergence of this function as another test case for the mixed-base scheme that is easier than the Collatz conjecture without being entirely trivial. Farkas defined this function as \begin{equation*}F^{\prime}(n)=\begin{cases}\frac{n}{3}&\text{if }n\equiv 0\pmod{3}\\ \frac{n+1}{2}&\text{if }n\not\equiv 0\pmod{3}\text{ and }n\equiv 1\pmod{4}\\ \frac{3n+1}{2}&\text{if }n\not\equiv 0\pmod{3}\text{ and }n\equiv 3\pmod{4}.\end{cases}\end{equation*} \par In this paper, we define another function $F\colon\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}$ that resembles the Collatz function more closely than Farkas' $F^{\prime}$ (with respect to the definitions of the cases) while being equivalent to $F^{\prime}$ in terms of convergence. It is obtained by introducing an additional case in the Collatz function for $n\equiv 1\pmod{3}$ and applying $T$ otherwise: \begin{equation*}F(n)=\begin{cases}\frac{n-1}{3}&\text{if }n\equiv 1\pmod{3}\\ \frac{n}{2}&\text{if }n\equiv 0\text{ or }n\equiv 2\pmod{6}\\ \frac{3n+1}{2}&\text{if }n\equiv 3\text{ or }n\equiv 5\pmod{6}\end{cases}\end{equation*} Given an $F^{\prime}$-trajectory, the bijection $n\mapsto(n-1)/2$ applied to each of its iterates maps the trajectory to the corresponding $F$-trajectory. Thus, an $F^{\prime}$-trajectory reaches $1$ if and only if the corresponding $F$-trajectory reaches $0$. Furthermore, an $F$-trajectory reaches $0$ if and only if it reaches $1$, which is a convenient fact since in our string representations we do not represent $0$, so in order to prove that $F$ is convergent it suffices to show that for all $n\in\mathbb{N}^{+}$ the trajectory $F^{\mathbb{N}}(n)$ contains $1$. \par Note that $F$ can also be viewed as a generalized Collatz function with 5 cases, which makes it straightforward to translate the cases of $F$ into a set $\mathcal{D}_{F}$ of dynamic rules (shown below) to obtain a rewriting system that simulates the iterated application of $F$ on positive integers. \begin{equation*}\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}\end{array}\end{equation*} Termination of the rewriting system $\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{D}_{F}\cup\mathcal{X}$ is equivalent to the convergence of $F$. The proof of the equivalence is essentially the same as that of \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{thm:simulation} except for the step where we construct a nonterminating rewrite sequence from a nonconvergent trajectory. To construct such a rewrite sequence for $\mathcal{F}$, we take any nonconvergent $F$-trajectory and write the first number in the trajectory in \emph{ternary} (instead of binary). If the leading digit turns out to be $2_{3}$ we replace it by $1_{0}0_{2}$ since the leading digit is always assumed to be $1_{0}$ in our mixed-base representations. Then repeatedly performing the rightmost possible rewrite results in a rewrite sequence that simulates the $F$-trajectory. \par Farkas gave an inductive proof of convergence for $F^{\prime}$ via case analysis. We found an automated proof that $\mathcal{F}$ is terminating via arctic matrix interpretations. Below, we present both a manual proof of convergence for $F$ and the automated proof of $\SN(\mathcal{F})$. It is worth mentioning that the default configurations of the existing termination tools (e.g., {AProVE}{}, {Matchbox}{}) are too conservative to prove the termination of this system, but after their authors tweaked the strategies they were also able to find automated proofs via arctic matrix interpretations. It also remains an interesting question whether the automated proof somehow captures the argument in the inductive proof. \par\begin{theorem}For all $n\in\mathbb{N}^{+}$, the trajectory $F^{\mathbb{N}}(n)$ contains $1$. \end{theorem} \@proof[Proof (in the manner of Farkas~{\cite[Theorem~1]{Far05}})] We proceed by induction. For $n=1$ the result holds since $F^{\mathbb{N}}(1)\ =(1,0,0,\dots)$. Assuming it holds for all positive integers less than $n$, we will show that it holds for $n$. In particular, we will show that the trajectory of $n$ reaches a number strictly smaller than $n$, which implies by the induction hypothesis that the trajectory contains $1$. We split into three cases. \begin{enumerate}\enumerate@item@Assume $n\equiv 1\pmod{3}$. Then $F(n)=(n-1)/3$ is smaller than $n$. \par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@Assume $n\equiv 0$ or $n\equiv 2\pmod{6}$. Then $F(n)=n/2$ is smaller than $n$. \par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@Assume $n\equiv 3$ or $n\equiv 5\pmod{6}$. Denote $n$ by $N_{1}$, and consider the trajectory $F^{\mathbb{N}}(N_{1})=(N_{1},N_{2},N_{3},\dots)$. Let \begin{equation*}k=\sup\left\{i\in\mathbb{N}^{+}\mathrel{\bigg|}N_{j}=\frac{3N_{j-1}+1}{2}\text{ for all }1<j\leq i\right\}.\end{equation*}We cannot have $k=\infty$, i.e., $F$ cannot apply $N\mapsto(3N+1)/2$ indefinitely, since for such a sequence we have \begin{equation*}N_{j}+1=\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)(N_{j-1}+1)=\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)^{j-1}(N_{1}+1).\end{equation*}As all the elements in the trajectory have to be integers, $k-1$ cannot exceed the number of times that $N_{1}+1$ can be divided by $2$, so $k$ is finite. Consider \begin{equation*}N_{k+1}=\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)^{k}(N_{1}+1)-1.\end{equation*}It is of the form $3^{k}\cdot L-1$ for some positive integer $L$, so we know that either $N_{k+1}\equiv 2\pmod{6}$ or $N_{k+1}\equiv 5\pmod{6}$. Due to the way $k$ is defined, $N_{k+1}$ cannot be congruent to $5\pmod{6}$, so we have $N_{k+1}\equiv 2\pmod{6}$. In particular, $N_{k+1}$ is even, so $\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)^{k}(N_{1}+1)$ is odd, which in turn implies that $\frac{N_{1}+1}{2^{k}}$ is odd, i.e., we have $N_{1}=2^{k}\cdot(2M+1)-1$ for some natural number $M$. Then, $N_{k+1}$ satisfies \begin{equation*}N_{k+1}=3^{k}\cdot(2M+1)-1=3^{k}\cdot 2M+3^{k}-1.\end{equation*}Since $N_{k+1}\equiv 2\pmod{6}$, we can deduce that \begin{equation*}F(N_{k+1})=\frac{N_{k+1}}{2}=\frac{3^{k}\cdot 2M+3^{k}-1}{2}\equiv 1\pmod{3},\end{equation*}and furthermore, \begin{equation*}F^{2}(N_{k+1})=\frac{\frac{3^{k}\cdot 2M+3^{k}-1}{2}-1}{3}=3^{k-1}\cdot 2M+3^{k-1}-1\equiv 2\pmod{6}.\end{equation*}Repeatedly applying $F$ in the above manner gives $F^{2k}(N_{k+1})=2M$. Since $k\geq 1$, we have $2M<2^{k}\cdot(2M+1)-1=N_{1}=n$, and so the trajectory $F^{\mathbb{N}}(n)$ reaches a number strictly smaller than $n$. \qed\end{enumerate} \end@proof \@proof[Proof (via arctic matrix interpretations)] We will show $\SN(\mathcal{F})$.
