By \lx@cref{creftypeplural\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:A-termination} and\lx@nobreakspace\lx@cref{refnum}{lem:reversal}, we have $\SN({\mathcal{X}}^{\mathrm{rev}})$. Consider the arctic matrix interpretations below. Recall that $-$ indicates $-\infty$ in the matrices. Also recall that arctic addition is $\max$ and arctic multiplication is $+$. { \NiceMatrixBlock[auto-columns-width] \begin{equation}\begin{gathered}(\mathbf{x})=\begin{bNiceMatrix}\mathbf{x}\oplus\begin{bNiceMatrix}\qquad[\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}](\mathbf{x})=\begin{bNiceMatrix}\mathbf{x}\oplus\begin{bNiceMatrix}\\ [\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}](\mathbf{x})=\begin{bNiceMatrix}\qquad[\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}](\mathbf{x})=\begin{bNiceMatrix}\mathbf{x}\\ [\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}](\mathbf{x})=\begin{bNiceMatrix}\mathbf{x}\qquad[\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}](\mathbf{x})=\begin{bNiceMatrix}\mathbf{x}\qquad[\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}}](\mathbf{x})=\begin{bNiceMatrix}\mathbf{x}\end{gathered}\end{equation} }Above interpretations prove $\SN({\mathcal{D}_{F}}^{\mathrm{rev}}_{\mathrm{top}}\mathbin{/}{\mathcal{X}}^{\mathrm{rev}})$ by \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{thm:monotone-algebra}. (See \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{sec:farkas-interpretations} for the corresponding interpretations of the rules of ${\mathcal{F}}^{\mathrm{rev}}$.) We then get $\SN({\mathcal{D}_{F}}^{\mathrm{rev}}\mathbin{/}{\mathcal{X}}^{\mathrm{rev}})$ by \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:T-top-termination}. As we know that ${\mathcal{X}}^{\mathrm{rev}}$ is terminating, by \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{thm:relative-termination} we can conclude $\SN({\mathcal{D}_{F}}^{\mathrm{rev}}\cup{\mathcal{X}}^{\mathrm{rev}})$, which implies $\SN(\mathcal{F})$ via \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:reversal}. \qed\end@proof \par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Subsets of the Collatz System} \par It is also interesting to consider whether we can automatically prove termination of proper subsets of $\mathcal{T}$. Specifically, we considered the 11 subsystems obtained by leaving out a single rewriting rule from $\mathcal{T}$, and we found termination proofs via matrix interpretations for all of the 11 subproblems. \par The reason for our interest in these problems is threefold: \begin{enumerate}\enumerate@item@Termination of $\mathcal{T}$ implies termination of all of its subsystems, so proving its termination is at least as difficult a task as proving termination of the 11 subsystems. Therefore, the subproblems serve as additional sanity checks that an automated approach aspiring to succeed for the Collatz conjecture ought to be able to pass. Furthermore, they form an additional source of weakened variants that give us a heuristic idea of the suitable choice of parameters to use when searching for interpretations for the full problem. \par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@When proving termination in a stepwise manner, we solve a sequence of relative termination problems. Having proved termination of all 11 subsystems is a partial solution to the full problem, since it implies that for any single rule $\ell\to r\in\mathcal{T}$, proving $\SN(\{\ell\to r\}\mathbin{/}\mathcal{T})$ settles the Collatz conjecture. \par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@After the removal of a rule, the termination of the remaining system still encodes a valid mathematical question about the Collatz trajectories. The question of the termination of a proper subset is equivalent to asking if every corresponding Collatz trajectory that does not require the use of the left-out rule is convergent. For instance, removing $\{\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}\to\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}\}$ deletes the odd case of the Collatz function, which gives a clearly convergent function. Removing the auxiliary rules in $\mathcal{X}$ lead to potentially more interesting questions: For instance, the termination of $\mathcal{T}\setminus\{\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}\to\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\}$ implies that the trajectories where the pattern $\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}$ is never encountered during the simulation are all convergent. \par Note that this type of inquiry is less meaningful for the unary system $\mathcal{Z}$ as its simulation of the Collatz function depends crucially on each rule being present. Leaving out some subset of the rules from $\mathcal{Z}$ causes the system to become terminating for a trivial reason, i.e., the computation it expresses no longer corresponds to the iterated application of some function. \end{enumerate} \par\begin{example}As an instance of leaving out a rule, consider the subsystem $\mathcal{T}\setminus\{\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}\to\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\}$. There is a single-step natural matrix interpretations proof that this system is terminating: { \@@amsgather }It can be checked that under these interpretations the rules of $\mathcal{T}\setminus\{\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}\to\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\}$ satisfy the following relations: \NiceMatrixBlock[auto-columns-width] \begin{longtable}[]{IJK}{[}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}](\mathbf{x})=\begin{bNiceMatrix}\mathbf{x}+\begin{bNiceMatrix}&>&\begin{bNiceMatrix}\mathbf{x}+\begin{bNiceMatrix}={[}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}](\mathbf{x})\\[18.24998pt] {[}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}](\mathbf{x})=\begin{bNiceMatrix}\mathbf{x}+\begin{bNiceMatrix}&>&\begin{bNiceMatrix}\mathbf{x}+\begin{bNiceMatrix}={[}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}](\mathbf{x})\\[18.24998pt] {[}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}](\mathbf{x})=\begin{bNiceMatrix}\mathbf{x}+\begin{bNiceMatrix}&>&\begin{bNiceMatrix}\mathbf{x}+\begin{bNiceMatrix}={[}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}](\mathbf{x})\\[18.24998pt] 0.0pt7.51s\@add@raggedleft&4&3&\hskip 0.0pt4.04s\@add@raggedleft\\ \hline\cr\hline\cr\end{tabular}\@add@centering\end{table}\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Odd Trajectories} \par In the originally defined Collatz function $C$, applying $2n+1\mapsto 6n+4$ produces an even number, so we incorporate a single division by $2$ into the definition of the odd case and obtain the function $T$ with the same overall dynamics as $C$. Taking this idea further by performing as many divisions by $2$ as possible leads to the so-called Syracuse function $\mathrm{Syr}\colon{2\mathbb{N}+1}\to{2\mathbb{N}+1}$, defined as \begin{equation*}\mathrm{Syr}(n)=\frac{3n+1}{2^{k}}\text{, where }k=\max\{k\in\mathbb{N}^{+}\mid 2^{k}\text{ divides }3n+1\}.\end{equation*} The Syracuse function maps each odd number to the next odd number in its Collatz trajectory. In this sense, it has the same overall dynamics as the Collatz functions $C$ or $T$ we have previously defined, i.e., $\mathrm{Syr}$ is convergent if and only if $C$ or $T$ is convergent. The function $T$ takes fewer steps than $C$ to reach $1$ and can be thought of as an accelerated Collatz function, making the Syracuse function a further accelerated version. Reducing the number of steps towards convergence is of interest mainly because the existence of various kinds of termination proofs is dependent on the derivational complexity of the rewriting system staying below certain thresholds\penalty\ \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{Number}{HL89}{}{}]}. For instance, given a rewriting system, if its termination can be proved in a single-step via natural matrix interpretations then its derivational complexity is at most exponential\penalty\ \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{Number}{EWZ08}{}{}]}. Similarly, a single-step termination proof via arctic matrix interpretations implies a linear derivational complexity\penalty\ \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{Number}{KW09}{}{}]}. Thus, it can be preferable to work with an alternative rewriting system with smaller derivational complexity that still simulates the dynamics of the Collatz function. \par Expressing the Syracuse function as a generalized Collatz function would require infinitely many cases to account for all of the possible appearances of $2^{k}$ as the denominator with different values of $k$. As a result, we are unable to simulate it with a finite rewriting system. Nevertheless, we may compromise and accelerate the Collatz function by a smaller amount. We first observe that if $n\equiv 1\pmod{8}$ then $\mathrm{Syr}(n)=\frac{3n+1}{4}$ and if $n\equiv 3\pmod{4}$ then $\mathrm{Syr}(n)=\frac{3n+1}{2}$. Furthermore, for any $n\in\mathbb{N}$ we have $\mathrm{Syr}(8n+5)=\mathrm{Syr}(2n+1)$ since $3(8n+5)+1=24n+16=4(6n+4)=4(3(2n+1)+1)$. Putting these observations together, we can define a generalized Collatz function $S\colon{2\mathbb{N}+1}\to{2\mathbb{N}+1}$ as follows. \begin{equation*}S(n)=\begin{cases}\frac{3n+1}{4}&\text{if }n\equiv 1\pmod{8}\\ \frac{n-1}{4}&\text{if }n\equiv 5\pmod{8}\\ \frac{3n+1}{2}&\text{if }n\equiv 3\pmod{4}\end{cases}\end{equation*} \begin{example}The three trajectories below illustrate how the dynamics of $C$, $\mathrm{Syr}$, and $S$ compare to one another. \begin{itemize}\itemize@item@$C^{\mathbb{N}}(19)=(19,58,29,88,44,22,11,34,17,52,26,13,40,20,10,5,16,8,4,2,1,4,2,1,\dots)$ \par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@$\mathrm{Syr}^{\mathbb{N}}(19)=(19,29,11,17,13,5,1,1,\dots)$ \par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@$S^{\mathbb{N}}(19)=(19,29,\mathbf{7},11,17,13,\mathbf{3},5,1,1,\dots)$ \end{itemize}Note that a $\mathrm{Syr}$-trajectory simply consists of the odd numbers in the corresponding $C$-trajectory; however, an $S$-trajectory is not necessarily contained in the corresponding $C$-trajectory. This is due to the $n\equiv 5\pmod{8}$ case of $S$ that maps $n$ to a number not necessarily contained in the $C$-trajectory (marked in bold above). Once one of the other two cases is encountered, the $S$-trajectory arrives back at the number $\mathrm{Syr}(n)$. \end{example} $S$ is convergent if and only if $C$ (or $T$) is convergent, and the number of steps that $S$ takes to converge is between that of $T$ and $\mathrm{Syr}$. In a manner similar to before, we can translate the cases of $S$ into a set $\mathcal{D}_{S}$ of dynamic rules (shown below). Since we are working with odd numbers we use a new symbol $\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\bullet}}$ to mark the end of a string, viewed functionally as $\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\bullet}}(x)=2x+1$. \begin{equation*}\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\bullet}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\bullet}}\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\bullet}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\bullet}}\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\bullet}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\bullet}}\\ \end{array}\end{equation*} Termination of the rewriting system $\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{D}_{S}\cup\mathcal{X}$ is equivalent to the convergence of $S$. Similar to $\mathcal{T}$, proving the termination of $\mathcal{S}$ is currently beyond our reach, although it may potentially be an easier path to the Collatz conjecture (compared to proving $\SN(\mathcal{T})$). Failing to prove the termination of $\mathcal{S}$ itself, we considered the subsystems of $\mathcal{S}$ as we did for $\mathcal{T}$ in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{sec:subsystems-T}. With matrix interpretations, termination of all but two of the 11-rule subsystems of $\mathcal{S}$ was automatically proved. Despite devoting thousands of CPU hours, we were not able to find interpretations to prove that $\mathcal{S}_{1}=\mathcal{S}\setminus\{\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\bullet}}\to\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\bullet}}\}$ or $\mathcal{S}_{2}=\mathcal{S}\setminus\{\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\bullet}}\to\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\bullet}}\}$ is terminating. By the discussion in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{sec:subsystems-T}, termination of subsystems of $\mathcal{S}$ corresponds to questions about the trajectories of $S$. In particular, for $\mathcal{S}_{1}$ and $\mathcal{S}_{2}$ we have the following. \begin{proposition}\begin{enumerate}\enumerate@item@$\SN(\mathcal{S}_{1})$ if and only if all nonconvergent $S$-trajectories contain some $n\equiv 1\pmod{8}$. \par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@$\SN(\mathcal{S}_{2})$ if and only if all nonconvergent $S$-trajectories contain some $n\equiv 5\pmod{8}$. \end{enumerate}\end{proposition} We can prove automatically via matrix interpretations that $\mathcal{S}_{3}=\mathcal{S}\setminus\{\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\bullet}}\to\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\bullet}}\}$ is terminating, which implies the following through an equivalence of the above form for $\mathcal{S}_{3}$. \begin{proposition}All nonconvergent $S$-trajectories contain some $n\equiv 3\pmod{4}$. \end{proposition} \par Leaving out some dynamic rules from a mixed-base rewriting system that simulates a generalized Collatz function allows us to phrase the above kinds of questions as termination problems. Recall that, although $S$ is equivalent to $C$ in terms of convergence, its trajectories are not necessarily contained in the Collatz trajectories, so we cannot substitute $C$ for $S$ in the above statements (except for the second item of \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{prop:s-convergence}, since the left-out rule is the one that maps to a number not contained in the Collatz trajectory). In the next section, we perform the same kind of inquiry into the original Collatz trajectories. \par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Collatz Trajectories Modulo \texorpdfstring{$8$}{8}} \@add@centering\end{figure} \par Our initial motivation for the previous section was to find rewriting systems with reduced derivational complexity. This inquiry led us to surprisingly difficult Collatz-like problems, which we believe may be on the border of the reach of automated methods. In this section we explore more problems of a similar form and present the unsolved ones as potentially reachable targets for automated termination proving. \par Let $m$ be a power of $2$. Given $k\in\{0,1,\dots,m-1\}$, is it the case that all nonconvergent Collatz trajectories contain some $n\equiv k\pmod{m}$? For several values of $k$ this can be proved by inspecting the transitions of the iterates in the Collatz trajectories across residue classes modulo $m$ (shown on \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{fig:modulo} for $m=8$). As an example, consider the case $k=6$. \par\begin{proposition}All nonconvergent $C$-trajectories contain some $n\equiv 6\pmod{8}$. \end{proposition} \@proof Let $t$ be a $C$-trajectory. The path that $t$ takes over the transition graph of $C$ in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{fig:modulo} is a concatenation of the simple cycles of the graph. If $t$ does not contain any $n\equiv 6\pmod{8}$, then the cycles containing $6$ cannot occur in the path, which leaves eight cycles (listed below). By composing the functions applied at each transition in the graph, we can compute the mapping (call it $c$) applied to an iterate after traversing a cycle: \par\begin{center}\begin{tabular}[]{@{}p{6em}ll}\hline\cr\hline\cr Cycle&$c(n)$\\ \hline\cr$(0,4,2,5,0)$&$(3n+8)/8$\\ $(0,0)$&$n/2$\\ $(1,4,2,1)$&$(3n+1)/4$\\ $(2,5,0,4,2)$&$(3n+2)/8$\\ $(2,1,4,2)$&$(3n+2)/4$\\ $(4,2,5,0,4)$&$(3n+4)/8$\\ $(4,2,1,4)$&$(3n+4)/4$\\ $(5,0,4,2,5)$&$(3n+1)/8$\\ \hline\cr\hline\cr\end{tabular}\end{center} \par For $t$ to be nonconvergent, it needs to contain infinitely many occurrences of cycles that increase the values of the iterates. All of the above cycles decrease the values of the iterates, so each iterate of $t$ eventually reaches a smaller number, implying $t$ is convergent. \qed\end@proof \par Propositions of the above kind can also be formulated as termination of some rewriting systems. With this approach we found automated proofs for several cases. \par We start by studying the smallest interesting case $m=4$. Consider the following two sets of dynamic rules to simulate the Collatz function. \begin{equation*}\mathcal{D}_{C}^{\mathrm{Even}/4}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}\end{array}\right\}\qquad\mathcal{D}_{T}^{\mathrm{Odd}/4}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}\end{array}\right\}\end{equation*} It is straightforward to see that the SRS $\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{Even}/4}=\mathcal{D}_{C}^{\mathrm{Even}/4}\cup\mathcal{X}$ simulates the function $C$ and the SRS $\mathcal{T}^{\mathrm{Odd}/4}=\mathcal{D}_{T}^{\mathrm{Odd}/4}\cup\mathcal{X}$ simulates the function $T$. The first two rules of $\mathcal{D}_{C}^{\mathrm{Even}/4}$ express the application of $C$ to numbers of the form $4n$ and $4n+2$. Similarly, the last two rules of $\mathcal{D}_{T}^{\mathrm{Odd}/4}$ express the application of $T$ to numbers of the form $4n+1$ and $4n+3$. Observing that a $T$-trajectory contains the same odd numbers as the corresponding $C$-trajectory, we have the following equivalences. \begin{proposition}\begin{enumerate}\enumerate@item@$\SN(\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{Even}/4}\setminus\{\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}\to\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}\})$ if and only if all nonconvergent $C$-trajectories contain some $n\equiv 0\pmod{4}$. \par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@$\SN(\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{Even}/4}\setminus\{\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}\to\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}\})$ if and only if all nonconvergent $C$-trajectories contain some $n\equiv 2\pmod{4}$. \par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@$\SN(\mathcal{T}^{\mathrm{Odd}/4}\setminus\{\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}\to\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}\})$ if and only if all nonconvergent $C$-trajectories contain some $n\equiv 1\pmod{4}$. \par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@$\SN(\mathcal{T}^{\mathrm{Odd}/4}\setminus\{\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}\to\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}\})$ if and only if all nonconvergent $C$-trajectories contain some $n\equiv 3\pmod{4}$. \end{enumerate}\end{proposition} We were able to prove termination of the above four systems via matrix interpretations, thus establishing the following. \begin{theorem}If there exists a nonconvergent Collatz trajectory, it cannot avoid any of the residue classes modulo $4$. \end{theorem} \par Next, we look at the case $m=8$. It can be studied via the analogously defined rewriting systems: \begin{equation*}\mathcal{D}_{C}^{\mathrm{Even}/8}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}\end{array}\right\}\qquad\mathcal{D}_{T}^{\mathrm{Odd}/8}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}\end{array}\right\}\end{equation*} The SRS $\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{Even}/8}=\mathcal{D}_{C}^{\mathrm{Even}/8}\cup\mathcal{X}$ simulates the function $C$ and the SRS $\mathcal{T}^{\mathrm{Odd}/8}=\mathcal{D}_{T}^{\mathrm{Odd}/8}\cup\mathcal{X}$ simulates the function $T$. We do not formally state the analogue of \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{prop:mod4-equiv} --- terminations of the eight subsystems obtained from $\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{Even}/8}$ or $\mathcal{T}^{\mathrm{Odd}/8}$ are each equivalent to the question of whether all nonconvergent Collatz trajectories have to encounter the corresponding residue class modulo $8$. Through automated proofs, we were able to establish the following. \begin{theorem}If there exists a nonconvergent Collatz trajectory, it cannot avoid the residue classes of $2$, $3$, $4$, $6$ modulo $8$. \end{theorem} \lx@cref{creftypeplural\lx@tilde refnum}{thm:collatz-mod4} and\lx@nobreakspace\lx@cref{refnum}{thm:collatz-2346mod8} are also not difficult to prove by hand; however, it remains open whether an analogue of \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{thm:collatz-2346mod8} holds for the residue classes of $0$, $1$, $5$, $7$ modulo $8$, that is, we have neither manual nor automated proofs for these cases. They may serve well as easier Collatz-like open problems to test our abilities against, so we offer a \$500 reward for a resolution of any of the following conjectures. \begin{conjecture}All nonconvergent $C$-trajectories contain some $n\equiv 1\pmod{8}$. \end{conjecture} \begin{conjecture}All nonconvergent $C$-trajectories contain some $n\equiv 5\pmod{8}$. \end{conjecture} \begin{conjecture}All nonconvergent $C$-trajectories contain some $n\equiv 7\pmod{8}$. \end{conjecture} Even the following easier statement remains unproved, although we were able to prove (both manually and automatically) its versions with $\{1,5\}$ and $\{1,7\}$ as the residue classes. \begin{conjecture}All nonconvergent $C$-trajectories contain either some $n\equiv 5\pmod{8}$ or some $n\equiv 7\pmod{8}$. \end{conjecture} \par We can alternatively formulate each of the above conjectures as the convergence problem of some partial generalized Collatz function. For instance, \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{conj:5or7mod8} is equivalent to the convergence of the function $H\colon\mathbb{N}^{+}_{\bot}\to\mathbb{N}^{+}_{\bot}$ defined as follows. \begin{equation}H(n)=\begin{cases}\frac{3n}{4}&\text{if }n\equiv 0\pmod{4}\\ \frac{9n+1}{8}&\text{if }n\equiv 7\pmod{8}\\ \bot&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}\end{equation} \begin{proposition}$H$ is convergent if and only if all nonconvergent $C$-trajectories contain either some $n\equiv 5\pmod{8}$ or some $n\equiv 7\pmod{8}$. \end{proposition} \@proof Recall from \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{fig:modulo} the transition graph of the iterates in $C$-trajectories across residue classes modulo $8$. Observe that if a nonconvergent trajectory avoids the residue classes of $5$ and $7\pmod{8}$, then it has to visit the residue class of $1\pmod{8}$, so we may assume without loss of generality that each nonconvergent trajectory starts with some $n\equiv 1\pmod{8}$. \par Then a nonconvergent trajectory that avoids the residue classes of $5$ and $7\pmod{8}$ can traverse only the cycle of classes $(1,4,2,1)$ or $(1,4,6,3,2,1)$. The former, which is traversed only if $n\equiv 1\pmod{32}$, effects the map $n\mapsto(3n+1)/4$. The latter, which is traversed only if $n\equiv 57\pmod{64}$, effects the map $n\mapsto(9n+7)/8$. If $n$ belongs to any other residue class, either the trajectory is convergent, or it contains some $k\equiv 5\pmod{8}$ or some $k\equiv 7\pmod{8}$. As a result, the function $G\colon\mathbb{N}^{+}_{\bot}\setminus\{1\}\to\mathbb{N}^{+}_{\bot}\setminus\{1\}$ defined as \begin{equation*}G(n)=\begin{cases}\frac{3n+1}{4}&\text{if }n\equiv\phantom{5}1\pmod{32}\\ \frac{9n+7}{8}&\text{if }n\equiv 57\pmod{64}\\ \bot&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}\end{equation*} is nonconvergent if and only if there exists a nonconvergent $C$-trajectory that avoids the residue classes of $5$ and $7\pmod{8}$. Moreover, $H$ as defined in\penalty\ \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:fn-5or7mod8} is convergent if and only if $G$ is convergent, because the bijection $n\mapsto(n-1)/8$ maps a $G$-trajectory to an $H$-trajectory. \qed\end@proof \par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{SAT Solving Considerations} \par\par\@@unnumbered@section{paragraph}{toc}{Phase-saving heuristic.} Most top-tier termination tools, such as {AProVE}{}, {Matchbox}{}, and {T\kern-1.49994pt\raisebox{-2.44444pt}{T}\kern-1.49994ptT\kern-1.49994pt\raisebox{-2.44444pt}{2}}{}, use the SAT solver {MiniSat}\penalty\ \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{Number}{ES04}{}{}]} to search for matrix interpretations. This choice is somewhat surprising as {MiniSat}{} has not been updated since 2008 and the performance of SAT solvers has improved significantly in the last decade. The use of {MiniSat}{} in these provers is motivated by its observed effectiveness in finding interpretations. We investigated the reason for this, which turned out to be a heuristic that {MiniSat}{} disables in its default configuration. \par Most SAT solvers sort variables by the frequency of their occurrences in recent conflict clauses, selecting the highest ranked one as the variable to branch on\penalty\ \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{Number}{MMZ+01}{}{}]}. This procedure is similar in {MiniSat}{} and modern SAT solvers. However, once a variable is picked, the solvers differ in the truth value assignment that they explore first. {MiniSat}{} uses negative branching\penalty\ \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{Number}{ES04}{}{}]}, which explores the ``false'' branch first for all decision variables. The empirical effectiveness of this heuristic likely stems from its interaction with the common choices of propositional encodings used when translating problems into SAT instances. Modern SAT solvers use phase-saving\penalty\ \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{Number}{PD07}{}{}]}, which first explores the branch corresponding to the truth value to which the variable was forced most recently during unit propagation. We offer the following explanation for the effectiveness of negative branching. \par We use the \emph{order encoding}\penalty\ \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{Number}{TTKB09,PJ11}{}{}]} when translating the integer constraints from \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{sec:natural-arctic-matrix-interpretations} into CNF formulas. In this encoding, each variable $X$ that takes values in a finite domain $A=\{0,1,\dots,n+1\}$ is represented by a corresponding list of $n+1$ Boolean variables $(x_{0},x_{1},\dots,x_{n})$. Each Boolean variable $x_{i}$ indicates $X>i$. As an example, $X=2$ has the corresponding assignment $(1,1,0,0,\dots,0)$. This encoding gives a natural way of representing inequality constraints. For instance, the constraint $a<X\leq b$ can be enforced simply by setting $x_{a}=1$ and $x_{b}=0$. As another example, for variables $X$, $Y$ the inequality $X\leq Y$ can be expressed by the conjunction $\bigwedge_{i=0}^{n}(\neg x_{i}\vee y_{i})$. Consequently, when using the order encoding, representations that correspond to smaller numbers require setting a larger fraction of the Boolean variables to false. For instance, the representation of zero or minus infinity assigns all variables to false. Moreover, the matrices and the vectors that occur in the interpretations are often sparse and contain relatively few large numbers. As a result, negative branching possibly directs the search towards a satisfying assignment more often. \par Additionally, in contrast to phase-saving, negative branching tends to guide the solver to a potentially different part of the search space after a restart (which is performed frequently in SAT solvers). The increased diversity of the search space (across restarts) that is explored due to negative branching is also possibly helpful for the kinds of the problems discussed in this paper. \par One can enable negative branching in most SAT solvers, which in our case improves solver performance for some of the formulas that encode the existence of interpretations. \par\par\@@unnumbered@section{paragraph}{toc}{Heavy-tailed behavior of SAT solvers.} Combinatorial search algorithms can have a tendency to show a large variance in runtime across different, randomly selected initial conditions of the search. For instance, Gomes\penalty\ et\penalty\ al.\penalty\ \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{Number}{GSCK00}{}{}]} observed that DPLL solvers\penalty\ \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{Number}{DP60,DLL62}{}{}]} had a substantial fraction of very short runs when dealing with relatively hard instances. This suggests that trying to solve such instances using several parallel runs is a reasonable strategy, since some of the runs will have a nonnegligible chance of finishing early. \par Modern SAT solvers incorporate heuristics that alleviate the heavy-tailed behavior to some extent; however, the variability in runtime (especially on satisfiable instances) is not completely eliminated. In our termination prover, we run multiple instances of the SAT solver (specifically, {CaDiCaL}{}\penalty\ \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{Number}{Bie19}{}{}]}) in parallel on the formula that encodes the existence of an interpretation. We introduce additional randomness to the procedure by running each parallel instance with a different shuffling of the clauses in the formula. Ideally, solver performance would be invariant to the ordering of the clauses; however, the clause order indirectly affects the variable selection heuristic, which ends up influencing runtime. \par\par\@@unnumbered@section{paragraph}{toc}{Experiments with solver configurations.} \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{table:sat-solving} shows the difference in runtime for both {MiniSat}{} and {CaDiCaL}{} due to negative branching and parallelism with a few small-scale experiments. From all the automated proofs in this paper, we selected the steps where the SAT solving phase took longer than 2 seconds, and reran the prover for these specific steps with different configurations of those SAT solvers. When running only a single instance of a solver, we used the nonshuffled formula. In the parallel case, we ran 8 solver instances with one of the instances receiving the nonshuffled formula and the other 7 receiving random shuffles. Running multiple instances of a solver in parallel is a considerable improvement in all cases where the allotted time is enough to find an interpretation. Negative branching has an adverse effect in some cases, although in two of the most difficult instances ($\mathcal{F}$ and $\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{Even}/8}\setminus\{\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}\to\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}\}$) it makes the difference between finding an interpretation relatively quickly and timing out. It also appears that, at least for the more difficult cases, replacing {MiniSat}{} by {CaDiCaL}{} can be beneficial. These experiments are not extensive enough to derive broad conclusions; however, they suggest that running multiple instances of a modern SAT solver with negative branching enabled in some and disabled in others can be a reasonably diverse strategy when searching for matrix interpretations. \begin{table}\centering{\begin{tabular}[]{l rrr c Q{4em}Q{4em} \par\par\@@numbered@section{section}{toc}{Related Work} There are several previous studies of the Collatz conjecture through alternative models of computation. \par\par\@@unnumbered@section{paragraph}{toc}{String rewriting systems.} To our knowledge, Zantema\penalty\ \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{Number}{Zan05}{}{}]}, with his system $\mathcal{Z}$, which we saw in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{sec:rewriting-unary}, was the first to attempt using an automated method and string rewriting to search for a proof of the Collatz conjecture. In addition, although we independently discovered the mixed binary--ternary system described in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{sec:rewriting-mixed-base}, Scollo\penalty\ \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{Number}{Sco05}{}{}]} had essentially the same idea, the distinction being that he adopted a functional view of the digits that is different than in\penalty\ \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:interp}. Scollo was not concerned with proving termination, though, and proposed rewriting primarily as a formalism that forgoes the arithmetic interpretation of the iterates and instead emphasizes the dynamic/computational behavior. \par\par\@@unnumbered@section{paragraph}{toc}{Tag systems.} An $m$-tag\penalty\ system\penalty\ \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{Number}{Pos43}{}{}]} is a computational model described by a set of production rules that map symbols to strings (or ``tags''). Given an initial string $X=x_{0}x_{1}\dots x_{n}$, the tag system finds the rule $x_{0}\rightharpoonup Z$ whose LHS matches the leftmost symbol of $X$, appends the tag $Z$ to $X$, and deletes the first $m$ symbols of $XZ$; resulting in a new string $X^{\prime}=x_{m}x_{m+1}\dots x_{n}Z$. The tag system repeats this transformation until reaching a string of length less than $m$, at which point it halts. \par De Mol\penalty\ \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{Number}{De08}{}{}]} showed the existence of a small $2$-tag system with the following rules that simulates the iterated application of the Collatz function given a unary representation: \begin{equation*}\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}&\rightharpoonup&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}&\rightharpoonup&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}&\rightharpoonup&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}\end{array}\end{equation*} This tag system halts if and only if the Collatz conjecture holds, giving yet another formulation of the problem. De Mol further extended this scheme to allow the simulation of an arbitrary generalized Collatz function with modulus $d$ by a $d$-tag system. \par\par\@@unnumbered@section{paragraph}{toc}{Cellular automata.} Kari\penalty\ \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{Number}{Kar12}{}{}]} designed 1D cellular automata that perform multiplication by $3$ and $3/2$ in base $6$, and reformulated both the Collatz conjecture and Mahler's $3/2$ problem as sets of constraints to be satisfied by the space-time diagrams of these cellular automata. \par\par\@@unnumbered@section{paragraph}{toc}{String arithmetic.} Kauffman\penalty\ \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{Number}{Kau95}{}{}]} developed a formalism to perform arithmetic, which he called \emph{string arithmetic}, and expressed the Collatz conjecture within it. This formalism works with unary representations of numbers, and uses the three symbols $\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}$, $\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}$, $\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}$. Letting $\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\epsilon}}$ denote the empty string and $\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{N}}$ be any string representing a number, string arithmetic consists of the following bidirectional rewrite rules (or ``identities'') to convert between different strings representing the same number: \begin{equation*}\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}&\xleftrightarrow{\makebox[5.97917pt]{}}&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\epsilon}}\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}&\xleftrightarrow{\makebox[5.97917pt]{}}&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{N}}&\xleftrightarrow{\makebox[5.97917pt]{}}&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{N}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}\end{array}\end{equation*} Along with the above identities, Kauffman's encoding of the Collatz function uses the following two rules: \begin{equation*}\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{N}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{N}}\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{N}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{N}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{N}}\end{array}\end{equation*} The Collatz conjecture becomes the question of whether for strings of $\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}$s of all lengths there exists a rewrite sequence using the five rules above to reach the string $\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}$. \par\par\@@numbered@section{section}{toc}{Conclusions} \par We presented an approach to prove convergence of instances of generalized Collatz functions by translating them into string rewriting systems and applying SAT solving to find matrix interpretations that show termination. Important components of our approach include the quality of the translation into rewriting, the use of weakly monotone $\Sigma$-algebras, and dedicated heuristics in the SAT solving phase. We considered some interesting, simpler variants of the Collatz conjecture to gauge the feasibility of this approach in proving mathematically interesting statements. We observed that some variants could be solved only via natural matrix interpretations, while others required arctic matrix interpretations. \par Several extensions to this work can further our understanding of the potential of rewriting techniques for answering mathematical questions. For instance, it is of interest to study the efficacy of different termination proving techniques on the problems that we considered. In particular, we found matrix interpretations to be the most successful for our purposes despite the existence of newer techniques developed for automatically proving termination of a few select challenging instances. Although matrix interpretations lead to automated proofs of several weakened variants discussed in this paper, it might still be the case that there exist no such interpretations to establish the termination of the Collatz system $\mathcal{T}$ (as we have shown in the case of natural matrix interpretations for Zantema's system $\mathcal{Z}$). This would be an interesting result in itself. Another issue is the matter of representation; specifically, it is worth exploring whether there exists a suitable translation of the Collatz conjecture into the termination of a term, instead of string, rewriting system since many automated termination proving techniques are generalized to term rewriting. Finally, injecting problem-specific knowledge into the rewriting systems or the termination techniques would be helpful as there exists a wealth of information about the Collatz conjecture that could simplify the search for a termination proof. \par \par\par\@@unnumbered@section{section}{Acknowledgments} We thank Johannes Waldmann for insightful discussions regarding arctic matrix interpretations, for pointing us to\penalty\ \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{Number}{Kar12}{}{}]} and the rewriting system in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{ex:waldmann-unary}, for responding to our challenge to solve Farkas' variant with {Matchbox}, and for feedback on an early draft. We thank Carsten Fuhs and J\"{u}rgen Giesl for responding to our challenge to solve Farkas' variant with {AProVE}{}. Springer, 2004. \par\reset@natbib@cites\@@lbibitem{WdE10}\NAT@@wrout{55}{10}{WdE}{WdE10}{WdE [10]}{WdE10}\lx@bibnewblock Johannes Waldmann, Roel {de Vrijer}, and J{\"{o}}rg Endrullis. \lx@bibnewblock Local termination: Theory and practice. \lx@bibnewblock{Logical Methods in Computer Science}, 6(3:20), 2010. \par\reset@natbib@cites\@@lbibitem{Zan94}\NAT@@wrout{56}{94}{Zan}{Zan94}{Zan [94]}{Zan94}\lx@bibnewblock Hans Zantema. \lx@bibnewblock Termination of term rewriting: Interpretation and type elimination. \lx@bibnewblock{Journal of Symbolic Computation}, 17(1):23--50, 1994. \par\reset@natbib@cites\@@lbibitem{Zan05}\NAT@@wrout{57}{05}{Zan}{Zan05}{Zan [05]}{Zan05}\lx@bibnewblock Hans Zantema. \lx@bibnewblock Termination of string rewriting proved automatically. \lx@bibnewblock{Journal of Automated Reasoning}, 34(2):105--139, 2005. \par\endthebibliography \par\par\@@numbered@section{appendix}{toc}{Alternative Proof of \texorpdfstring{\cref{lem:blockwise-termination}}{Lemma}} \par Let us recall the statement of the result before proceeding with the proof. \par See \ref{restatable:blockwise} \@proof Assume there exists some string $X\in\{\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}},\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}},\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}},\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}},\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}},\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}},\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}\}^{*}$ that admits an infinite rewrite sequence for $\mathcal{T}$. View $X$ as split into blocks delimited by $\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}$ or $\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}$, i.e., for some $k\in\mathbb{N}^{+}$, write \begin{equation*}X=N_{1}d_{1}N_{2}d_{2}\dots d_{k-1}N_{k},\end{equation*} where $d_{i}\in\{\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}},\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}\}$ for each $1\leq i<k$, and $N_{i}\in\{\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}},\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}},\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}},\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}},\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}}\}^{*}$ for each $1\leq i\leq k$. Any string containing $X$ also admits an infinite rewrite sequence, so instead consider $Z=d_{0}Xd_{k}$, written as \begin{equation*}Z=d_{0}N_{1}d_{1}N_{2}d_{2}\dots d_{k-1}N_{k}d_{k},\end{equation*} where $d_{0},d_{k}\in\{\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}},\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}\}$. \par As $\mathcal{T}$ is not terminating on $Z$, there exists some rewrite rule applicable to it. Consider an arbitrary block of $Z$ along with its neighbors (to which some rewrite rule of $\mathcal{T}$ applies), written as \begin{equation*}V=PcQdR,\end{equation*} where $c,d\in\{\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}},\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}\}$ and $P,Q,R\in\{\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}},\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}},\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}},\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}},\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}}\}^{*}$. Due to the shapes of the rules of $\mathcal{T}$, the string $V$ can be rewritten only into one of the following: \begin{equation*}\begin{array}[]{rclrrcl}V_{1}&=&\widetilde{P}cQdR&\qquad\text{ such that }&Pc&\to_{\mathcal{T}}&\widetilde{P}c\\[2.5pt] V_{2}&=&Pc\widetilde{Q}dR&\qquad\text{ such that }&cQd&\to_{\mathcal{T}}&c\widetilde{Q}d\\[2.5pt] V_{3}&=&PcQd\widetilde{R}&\qquad\text{ such that }&dR&\to_{\mathcal{T}}&d\widetilde{R}\end{array}\end{equation*} In any case, the delimiters are unchanged, and only a single block is affected by the rewrite. This means that any rewrite sequence that starts from $Z$ consists of several sequences that each operate entirely on some block of $Z$. Thus, since $Z$ has finitely many blocks, there exists some block that admits an infinite rewrite sequence. In particular, there exists a string $cNd$, where $c,d\in\{\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}},\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}\}$ and $N\in\{\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}},\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}},\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}},\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}},\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}}\}^{*}$, that can be rewritten indefinitely. We claim that this requires $c=\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}$ and $d=\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}$, so the string $cNd$ is of the canonical form $\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}(\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}|\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}|\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}|\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}|\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}})^{*}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}$. As shown below, the other cases are all terminating. \begin{enumerate}\enumerate@item@$\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}N\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}$: This string does not contain $\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}$, so it can be rewritten using only the rules in $\mathcal{T}\setminus\mathcal{B}$, but we know from \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:A-termination} that $\SN(\mathcal{T}\setminus\mathcal{B})$, so there can be no infinite rewrite sequence. \par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@$\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}N\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}$: This string does not contain $\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}$, so it can be rewritten using only the rules in $\mathcal{T}\setminus\mathcal{D}_{T}$, but we know from \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:A-termination} that $\SN(\mathcal{T}\setminus\mathcal{D}_{T})$, so there can be no infinite rewrite sequence. \par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@$\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}N\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}$: The SRS $\mathcal{T}$ contains no rules of the form $s\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}\to t\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}$ or $\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}s\to\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}t$ for any $s,t$, so this string can be rewritten using only the rules in $\mathcal{T}\setminus(\mathcal{B}\cup\mathcal{D}_{T})$. Since each subset of a terminating SRS is terminating, by \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:A-termination} we conclude $\SN(\mathcal{T}\setminus(\mathcal{B}\cup\mathcal{D}_{T}))$, so there can be no infinite rewrite sequence. \end{enumerate} This proves the contrapositive of the lemma statement. \qed\end@proof \par\par\@@numbered@section{appendix}{toc}{Remaining Part of the Automated Proof for Farkas' Variant} \par With the interpretations from\penalty\ \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:farkas-interpretations}, the rules of ${\mathcal{F}}^{\mathrm{rev}}$ satisfy the following relations: \par\NiceMatrixBlock[auto-columns-width] Problems to Approach via Rewriting} \par We adapt the mixed base scheme for several other open problems and reformulate them as termination of relatively small rewriting systems. \par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Mahler's \texorpdfstring{$3/2$}{3/2} Problem} \begin{definition}Let $\xi\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}$ be a positive real number. It is called a \emph{$Z$-number} if for all $k\in\mathbb{N}$ we have $\fracp\left(\xi\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)^{k}\right)<\frac{1}{2}$, where $\fracp(\cdot)$ denotes the fractional part of the number. \end{definition} \par Mahler\penalty\ \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{Number}{Mah68}{}{}]} conjectured that there are no $Z$-numbers. Moreover, he considered a generalized Collatz function $M\colon\mathbb{N}^{+}\to\mathbb{N}^{+}$, defined as follows. \begin{equation*}M(n)=\begin{cases}\frac{3n}{2}&\text{if }n\equiv 0\pmod{2}\\ \frac{3n+1}{2}&\text{if }n\equiv 1\pmod{2}\end{cases}\end{equation*} He related the behaviors of $M$-trajectories to the existence of $Z$-numbers: \begin{theorem}For $n\in\mathbb{N}^{+}$, if a $Z$-number exists in the interval $[n,n+1)$, then there is no $k\in\mathbb{N}$ for which $M^{k}(n)\equiv 3\pmod{4}$. \end{theorem} In order to formulate this as a termination problem, we split the odd case of $M$ into two and leave one undefined: \begin{equation*}M^{\prime}(n)=\begin{cases}\frac{3n}{2}&\text{if }n\equiv 0\pmod{2}\\ \frac{3n+1}{2}&\text{if }n\equiv 1\pmod{4}\\ \bot&\text{if }n\equiv 3\pmod{4}\end{cases}\end{equation*} Given $n\in\mathbb{N}^{+}$, if the trajectory ${M^{\prime}}^{\mathbb{N}}(n)$ contains $\bot$ then by the contrapositive of \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{thm:mahler} there is no $Z$-number in the interval $[n,n+1)$. When proving nonexistence, we may assume without loss of generality that a $Z$-number is at least $1$, since if $\xi\in(0,1)$ is a $Z$-number then $\xi\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)^{m}\in[1,\infty)$ is another one for sufficiently large $m\in\mathbb{N}$. Thus, the nonexistence of $Z$-numbers can be established by proving that $M^{\prime}$ is convergent, which is equivalent to the termination of the following rewriting system $\mathcal{M}$. In order to ensure termination at the case $n\equiv 3\pmod{4}$, there is no rule with the LHS $\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}$. \begin{equation*}\begin{array}[t]{rcl}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}\\ \end{array}\qquad\begin{array}[t]{rcl}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\end{array}\qquad\begin{array}[t]{rcl}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\end{array}\qquad\begin{array}[t]{rcl}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\end{array}\end{equation*} \par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Halting Problem for Busy Beavers} \par The busy beaver problem\penalty\ \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{Number}{Rad62}{}{}]} concerns finding binary-alphabet Turing machines with $n$ states that, when given an input tape of all $0$s, write the largest number of $1$s on the tape upon halting. For each $n$, a machine that achieves this is called an \emph{$n$-state Busy Beaver} (or BB-$n$ for short). Note that this definition only requires the machines to halt on all-$0$ inputs, leaving the behavior on other inputs unspecified and allowing them not to halt in general. \par Michel\penalty\ \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{Number}{Mic15}{}{}]} observed that for $n\in\{2,3,4\}$, the busy beaver machines are all \emph{total Turing machines}, i.e., they halt on all inputs, and moreover proved that they all simulate some generalized Collatz function. It is an open problem whether all busy beavers are total Turing machines. In particular, it is unknown whether the current BB-$5$ candidate is total. Michel showed that the BB-$5$ candidate simulates the following generalized Collatz function. \begin{equation*}B(n)=\begin{cases}\frac{5n+18}{3}&\text{if }n\equiv 0\pmod{3}\\ \frac{5n+22}{3}&\text{if }n\equiv 1\pmod{3}\\ \bot&\text{if }n\equiv 2\pmod{3}\end{cases}\end{equation*} \par Convergence of the above function can be studied via the termination of a corresponding rewriting system, although the translation is not as straightforward as the other generalized Collatz functions we have in this paper. Specifically, the mixed base scheme results in a compact rewriting system only when all the mappings $an+b\mapsto cn+d$ applied by the generalized Collatz function at hand satisfy $d<c$. This is not the case for the function $B$ above since it requires us to express $3n\mapsto 5n+6$ and $3n+1\mapsto 5n+9$. These mappings are expressed more easily by the help of unary representations, so we can have the following hybrid rewriting system $\mathcal{B}\mathcal{B}^{\prime}$ over the alphabet $\{\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{i}},\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{v}},\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}},\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}},\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}\}$ to simulate the function $B$, where the new symbol $\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{v}}$ has the functional view $\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{v}}(x)=5x$. \begin{equation*}\begin{array}[t]{rcl}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{i}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{v}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{i}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{i}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{i}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{v}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{i}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{i}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{i}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{i}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}\end{array}\qquad\begin{array}[t]{rcl}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{i}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{i}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{i}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{i}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{i}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{v}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{v}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{i}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{i}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{i}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{i}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{i}}\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{v}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{v}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}\end{array}\qquad\begin{array}[t]{rcl}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{i}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{i}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{v}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{i}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{i}}\\ \end{array}\end{equation*} We did not succeed in proving the termination of this rewriting system, which is not too surprising since the hybrid scheme does not even pass the test of proving the convergence of $W$. Alternatively, we can split the cases of $B$ further for the congruence classes modulo $3^{k}$ with $k>1$ until the iterated application of $B$ can either be expressed as $an+b\mapsto cn+d$ with $d<c$, or it reaches $\bot$. After we perform this procedure while ensuring that the number of newly introduced cases are kept to a minimum, we end up with the following mappings to simulate $B$ in an accelerated manner (similar to the idea in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{sec:odd-trajectories}). \begin{equation}\begin{array}[]{rcl c rcl}9n&&&\;\;\mapsto&25n&+&16\\ 9n&+&1&\;\;\mapsto&25n&+&21\\ 27n&+&6&\;\;\mapsto&125n&+&64\\ 27n&+&7&\;\;\mapsto&125n&+&71\\ 27n&+&16&\;\;\mapsto&125n&+&114\\ 81n&+&51&\;\;\mapsto&625n&+&459\\ 243n&+&78&\;\;\mapsto&3125n&+&1116\\ 243n&+&159&\;\;\mapsto&3125n&+&2159\end{array}\end{equation} Consider the symbols $\{\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}},\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}},\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}},\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{v}},\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{w}},\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{x}},\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{y}},\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{z}},\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}},\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}\}$ with the following functional views. \begin{equation*}\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}(x)&=&3x\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}(x)&=&3x+1\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}}(x)&=&3x+2\end{array}\qquad\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{v}}(x)&=&5x\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{w}}(x)&=&5x+1\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{x}}(x)&=&5x+2\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{y}}(x)&=&5x+3\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{z}}(x)&=&5x+4\end{array}\qquad\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}(x)&=&0\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}(x)&=&x\end{array}\end{equation*} With these symbols, we have the following mixed $\{3,5\}$-ary (ternary--quinary) system $\mathcal{B}\mathcal{B}$ the termination of which is equivalent to the convergence of $B$. The dynamic rules on the left implement the mappings from\penalty\ \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:bb5-mappings} and the rest are auxiliary rules that push ternary symbols towards the rightmost end of the string while preserving its value. We were unable to prove the termination of this system, so we include it as yet another challenge for automated termination proving. \begin{equation*}\begin{array}[t]{rcl}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{y}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{w}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{z}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{w}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{x}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{x}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{z}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{x}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{z}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{w}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{z}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{x}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{z}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{y}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{y}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{w}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{z}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{w}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{y}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{z}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{y}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{w}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{y}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{x}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{w}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{w}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{z}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}\end{array}\qquad\begin{array}[t]{rcl}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{v}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{v}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{w}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{v}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{x}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{v}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}}\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{y}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{w}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{z}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{w}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}\end{array}\qquad\begin{array}[t]{rcl}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{v}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{w}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}}\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{w}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{x}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{x}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{x}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{y}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{x}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}}\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{z}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{y}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}\end{array}\qquad\begin{array}[t]{rcl}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{v}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{y}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{w}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{y}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}}\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{x}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{z}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{y}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{z}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{z}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{z}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}}\end{array}\qquad\begin{array}[t]{rcl}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{v}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{w}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{x}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}}\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{y}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{z}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}\end{array}\end{equation*} \par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Ternary Expansions of \texorpdfstring{$2^n$}{2n}} \par Erd\H{o}s\penalty\ \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{Number}{Erd79}{}{}]} asked: When does the ternary expansion of $2^{n}$ omit the digit $2$? This is the case for $2^{0}=(1)_{3}$, $2^{2}=(11)_{3}$, and $2^{8}=(100111)_{3}$. He conjectured that it does not happen for $n>8$. This conjecture can be reformulated as the statement that the following rewriting system $\mathcal{E}$, where $\overleftarrow{\mathcal{X}}=\{r\to\ell\mid\ell\to r\in\mathcal{X}\}$, is terminating on all initial strings of the form $\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}^{8}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}^{+}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}$. \begin{equation*}\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}\end{array}\qquad\overleftarrow{\mathcal{X}}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}}\end{array}\qquad\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}}\end{array}\qquad\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}\\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{f}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{t}}&\to&\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}}\end{array}\right\}\end{equation*} Given a string that corresponds to the binary representation of a power of $2$, the inverted system $\overleftarrow{\mathcal{X}}$ essentially rewrites the string into ternary by pushing ternary symbols to the right without altering the value that the string represents. The two rules $\{\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}\to\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}},\ \mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}\to\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}\}$ remove the occurrences of the ternary digits $\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{0}}$ and $\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{1}}$ (but not $\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}}$). If the ternary expansion does not contain the digit $\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{2}}$ then all digits will be removed, resulting in the string $\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleleft}}\mathmakebox[.57em]{\mathtt{\triangleright}}$ that can then be rewritten to itself indefinitely. \par This problem, as described, is an instance of ``local termination''\penalty\ \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{Number}{WdE10}{}{}]} since it is concerned with termination on not all possible strings but a subset of them. We have not performed experiments with this system or local termination yet and we leave this for future work. \par \par\@add@PDF@RDFa@triples\par\end{document}\end{longtable}\end{longtable}\end{longtable}\end{longtable}
